            - generic [ref=e163]:
              - generic [ref=e164]:
                - heading "Highlights" [level=2] [ref=e165]
                - list [ref=e168]:
                  - listitem [ref=e169]:
                    - generic [ref=e170]: •
                    - generic [ref=e172]: NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases are one of the largest and most widespread types of enzymes.
                  - listitem [ref=e173]:
                    - generic [ref=e174]: •
                    - generic [ref=e176]: NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases catalyze a wide variety of redox reactions with many different substrates.
                  - listitem [ref=e177]:
                    - generic [ref=e178]: •
                    - generic [ref=e180]: Metabolic engineering approaches have successfully exploited NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases.
                  - listitem [ref=e181]:
                    - generic [ref=e182]: •
                    - generic [ref=e184]: Protein engineering and directed evolution are used to tailor the catalytic properties of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases.
              - generic [ref=e185]:
                - heading "Abstract" [level=2] [ref=e186]
                - generic [ref=e188]:
                  - text: NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases catalyze the reduction or oxidation of a substrate coupled to the oxidation or reduction, respectively, of a nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide cofactor NAD(P)H or NAD(P)
                  - superscript [ref=e189]: +
                  - text: . NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases catalyze a large variety of reactions and play a pivotal role in many central metabolic pathways. Due to the high activity, regiospecificity and stereospecificity with which they catalyze redox reactions, they have been used as key components in a wide range of applications, including substrate utilization, the synthesis of chemicals, biodegradation and detoxification. There is great interest in tailoring NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases to make them more suitable for particular applications. Here, we review the main properties and classes of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases, the types of reactions they catalyze, some of the main protein engineering techniques used to modify their properties and some interesting examples of their modification and application.
            - list [ref=e190]:
              - listitem [ref=e191]:
                - link "Previous article in issue" [ref=e413] [cursor=pointer]:
                  - /url: /science/article/pii/S1570963917302546
                  - img [ref=e414]
                  - generic [ref=e417]: Previous article in issue
              - listitem [ref=e197]:
                - link "Next article in issue" [ref=e418] [cursor=pointer]:
                  - /url: /science/article/pii/S1570963917302662
                  - generic [ref=e420]: Next article in issue
                  - img [ref=e421]
            - generic [ref=e204]:
              - heading "Keywords" [level=2] [ref=e205]
              - generic [ref=e206]: NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases;
              - generic [ref=e207]: Metabolic engineering;
              - generic [ref=e208]: Protein engineering;
              - generic [ref=e209]: Directed evolution;
              - generic [ref=e210]: Enzyme promiscuity;
              - generic [ref=e211]: Substrate specificity
            - generic [ref=e423]:
              - generic [ref=e424]:
                - generic [ref=e425]:
                  - heading "1. Introduction" [level=2] [ref=e426]
                  - generic [ref=e427]:
                    - generic [ref=e428]:
                      - text: Oxidoreductases (Enzyme Commission [EC] primary class 1) catalyze the oxidation of one chemical species (a reducing agent or electron donor) with the concurrent reduction of another (an oxidizing agent or electron acceptor) in the form A
                      - superscript [ref=e429]: −
                      - text: + B → A + B
                      - superscript [ref=e430]: −
                      - text: and comprise almost one third of all enzymatic activities registered in the BRaunschweig ENzyme Database, BRENDA (
                      - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e431] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0005"
                        - generic [ref=e432]: Fig. 1
                      - text: ). Oxidoreductases can act on a wide range of both organic substrates including alcohols, amines and ketones and inorganic substrates including small anions such as sulfite, and metals such as mercury.
                    - figure [ref=e433]:
                      - generic [ref=e434]:
                        - img "Fig. 1" [ref=e435]
                        - list [ref=e436]:
                          - listitem [ref=e437]:
                            - 'link "Download: Download high-res image (145KB)" [ref=e438] [cursor=pointer]':
                              - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S157096391730273X-gr1_lrg.jpg
                              - generic [ref=e440]: "Download: Download high-res image (145KB)"
                          - listitem [ref=e441]:
                            - 'link "Download: Download full-size image" [ref=e442] [cursor=pointer]':
                              - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S157096391730273X-gr1.jpg
                              - generic [ref=e444]: "Download: Download full-size image"
                      - paragraph [ref=e447]:
                        - text: Fig. 1. Distribution of enzymatic activities registered in BRENDA. Oxidoreductases constitute approximately 30% of all the BRENDA enzymatic activities, among which around 50% use NADH/NAD
                        - superscript [ref=e448]: +
                        - text: and/or NADPH/NADP
                        - superscript [ref=e449]: +
                        - text: as a cofactor. The number of enzymatic activities of each EC class, as well as the number of NADH, NADPH and NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases, were obtained from BRENDA by means of manual queries and plotted with R and the ggplot2 package.
                  - generic [ref=e450]:
                    - generic [ref=e451]:
                      - text: NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases are able to oxidize a substrate by transferring a hydride (H
                      - superscript [ref=e452]: −
                      - text: ) group to a nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide cofactor (either NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e453]: +
                      - text: or NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e454]: +
                      - text: ), resulting in the reduced form NADH or NADPH (
                      - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e455] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0010"
                        - generic [ref=e456]: Fig. 2
                      - text: ), and make up over 50% of all oxidoreductase activities registered in the BRENDA (
                      - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e457] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0005"
                        - generic [ref=e458]: Fig. 1
                      - text: ). There are over 150,000 different sequences annotated as or predicted to be NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases
                      - link "[1]" [ref=e459] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0005"
                        - generic [ref=e460]: "[1]"
                      - text: .
                    - figure [ref=e461]:
                      - generic [ref=e462]:
                        - img "Fig. 2" [ref=e463]
                        - list [ref=e464]:
                          - listitem [ref=e465]:
                            - 'link "Download: Download high-res image (225KB)" [ref=e466] [cursor=pointer]':
                              - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S157096391730273X-gr2_lrg.jpg
                              - generic [ref=e468]: "Download: Download high-res image (225KB)"
                          - listitem [ref=e469]:
                            - 'link "Download: Download full-size image" [ref=e470] [cursor=pointer]':
                              - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S157096391730273X-gr2.jpg
                              - generic [ref=e472]: "Download: Download full-size image"
                      - paragraph [ref=e475]:
                        - text: Fig. 2. Molecular formula of NAD(P)H and NAD(P)
                        - superscript [ref=e476]: +
                        - text: . The NADH molecule contains adenine and nicotinamide nucleosides linked by a pyrophosphate linkage. The nicotinamide ring is the acceptor/donor of the electrons and the C4 (red) of the nicotinamide ring is the acceptor/donor of the proton. NADPH differs from NADH in the substituent at the C2 position of the adenosine moiety, indicated as ‘R’ in the figure, which is a hydroxy in NADH and a phosphate in NADPH. This phosphate group does not alter the ability of the cofactor to transfer electrons. NAD
                        - superscript [ref=e477]: +
                        - text: is the oxidized form of NADH, and NADP
                        - superscript [ref=e478]: +
                        - text: is the oxidized form of NADPH.
                  - generic [ref=e479]:
                    - generic [ref=e480]:
                      - text: NADH/NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e481]: +
                      - text: and NADPH/NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e482]: +
                      - text: serve as pools of redox cofactors for the cell. The nicotinamide ring of NADH/NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e483]: +
                      - text: or NADPH/NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e484]: +
                      - text: is the part of the cofactor directly involved in the transfer of electrons during the reactions catalyzed by NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases, while the C4 carbon atom of the nicotinamide ring acts as the acceptor/donor of a proton
                      - link "[2]" [ref=e485] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0010"
                        - generic [ref=e486]: "[2]"
                      - text: . The addition of the phosphate to the 2′-OH group of the adenine ribose ring in NADPH/NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e487]: +
                      - text: does not modify the electron transport capability as it is located far from the electron transfer region (
                      - link "Fig. 2" [ref=e488] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0010"
                        - generic [ref=e489]: Fig. 2
                      - text: ). However, the phosphate group modifies the structure of the cofactor, which allows different enzymes to have different specificities for NADH/NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e490]: +
                      - text: and NADPH/NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e491]: +
                      - text: ", thereby allowing these to act as two equivalent but independent redox systems. This has a physiological function, allowing different a redox poise (degree of reduction of the cofactor pool) to be maintained in the two systems, and independent fluxes. Typically, at least in heterotrophs, enzymes of catabolic pathways use NADH/NAD"
                      - superscript [ref=e492]: +
                      - text: ", while NADPH/NADP"
                      - superscript [ref=e493]: +
                      - text: is the preferred cofactor for anabolism
                      - link "[3]" [ref=e494] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0015"
                        - generic [ref=e495]: "[3]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[4]" [ref=e496] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0020"
                        - generic [ref=e497]: "[4]"
                      - text: . The redox poise of the NADH/NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e498]: +
                      - text: pool depends upon the availability and redox state of external electron acceptors and of substrates. This variation in redox poise can be observed for example in
                      - emphasis [ref=e499]: Escherichia coli
                      - text: growing
                      - emphasis [ref=e500]: via
                      - text: aerobic respiration, anaerobic respiration, or anaerobic fermentation, where factors such as the oxygen availability when growing aerobically, or the redox potential of other electron acceptors used when growing anaerobically, affect the steady-state NADH/NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e501]: +
                      - text: ratio
                      - link "[5]" [ref=e502] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0025"
                        - generic [ref=e503]: "[5]"
                      - text: . In contrast, the poise of the NADPH/NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e504]: +
                      - text: pool is maintained in a more reduced state in order to more effectively provide reducing power for biosynthesis
                      - link "[6]" [ref=e505] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0030"
                        - generic [ref=e506]: "[6]"
                      - text: . Accordingly, NADH-dependence is more prevalent among oxidoreductases acting on smaller molecules, which include most substrates and products of catabolism (
                      - link "Fig. 3" [ref=e507] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0015"
                        - generic [ref=e508]: Fig. 3
                      - text: ). Interestingly, substrates of low molecular weight can be metabolized by a higher number of enzymatic activities, indicating that smaller substrates have a role as central hubs of redox metabolic networks (
                      - link "Fig. 3" [ref=e509] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0015"
                        - generic [ref=e510]: Fig. 3
                      - text: b). There is also another, smaller increase of both the absolute number of oxidoreductase activities (
                      - link "Fig. 3" [ref=e511] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0015"
                        - generic [ref=e512]: Fig. 3
                      - text: a) and the number of oxidoreductase activities per substrate for substrates of molecular weight between 700 and 1100 Da, which is partially due to substrates that need to be activated by binding to coenzyme A (CoA) before the corresponding oxidoreductase can act on them (
                      - link "Fig. 3" [ref=e513] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#f0015"
                        - generic [ref=e514]: Fig. 3
                      - text: ). Canonically the differing redox states of the two systems (NADH and NADPH) is achieved in heterotrophs using NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e515]: +
                      - text: "-dependent glycolysis and the NADP"
                      - superscript [ref=e516]: +
                      - text: "-dependent pentose phosphate pathway, but there are several variations and alternatives"
                      - link "[7]" [ref=e517] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0035"
                        - generic [ref=e518]: "[7]"
                      - text: ", and photoautotrophs generate NADPH using the light-dependent reactions of photosynthesis. The independence of the NADH/NAD"
                      - superscript [ref=e519]: +
                      - text: and NADPH/NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e520]: +
                      - text: pools also allows different enzymes with different redox cofactors to catalyze key steps of opposite pathways helping to prevent futile cycles
                      - link "[8]" [ref=e521] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0040"
                        - generic [ref=e522]: "[8]"
                      - text: .
                    - figure [ref=e523]:
                      - generic [ref=e524]:
                        - img "Fig. 3" [ref=e525]
                        - list [ref=e526]:
                          - listitem [ref=e527]:
                            - 'link "Download: Download high-res image (253KB)" [ref=e528] [cursor=pointer]':
                              - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S157096391730273X-gr3_lrg.jpg
                              - generic [ref=e530]: "Download: Download high-res image (253KB)"
                          - listitem [ref=e531]:
                            - 'link "Download: Download full-size image" [ref=e532] [cursor=pointer]':

                              - generic [ref=e534]: "Download: Download full-size image"
                      - paragraph [ref=e537]:
                        - text: Fig. 3. a. Number of oxidoreductase activities
                        - emphasis [ref=e538]: versus
                        - text: molecular/atomic weight of the substrates. Substrates are grouped in intervals of 100 Da. Some substrate examples are labelled. A list of enzymes was extracted from the KEGG database using R with the KEGGREST package by imposing the condition that either NAD(P)
                        - superscript [ref=e539]: +
                        - text: or NAD(P)H were involved in the reaction as substrate or product. Results were plotted with R and the ggplot2 package. b. Number of oxidoreductase activities per substrate
                        - emphasis [ref=e540]: versus
                        - text: molecular/atomic weight of the substrates. Data were obtained and plotted in the same way as for
                        - link "Fig. 3" [ref=e541] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#f0015"
                          - generic [ref=e542]: Fig. 3
                        - text: a, but the number of oxidoreductase activities in each range of molecular/atomic weight was divided by the total number of substrates in that interval.
                  - generic [ref=e543]:
                    - text: NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases are of great interest from an industrial point of view as they perform the critical steps in the production of many hard-to-synthesize compounds, under mild conditions. For example, even though several chemical methods have been developed to perform the oxidation of primary alcohols, they are usually laborious and can lead to the formation of toxic products
                    - link "[9]" [ref=e544] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0045"
                      - generic [ref=e545]: "[9]"
                    - text: ", which can be avoided through enzymatic catalysis. Additionally, NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases possess other properties which make them very attractive alternatives to organic chemical synthesis, such as stereospecificity, regiospecificity and the possibility to tailor them to have the appropriate kinetic parameters and the desired substrate specificity"
                    - link "[10]" [ref=e546] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0050"
                      - generic [ref=e547]: "[10]"
                    - text: . For these reasons, NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases have been used extensively in metabolic engineering for the production of low, mid and high value products
                    - link "[11]" [ref=e548] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0055"
                      - generic [ref=e549]: "[11]"
                    - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e550]:
                    - generic [ref=e551]:
                      - text: NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases are too numerous and diverse to review exhaustively. Therefore, the reader is provided with a list of recommended papers and reviews relevant to NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases (
                      - link "Table 1" [ref=e552] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#t0005"
                        - generic [ref=e553]: Table 1
                      - text: ). Here we review this important group of enzymes broadly, with an applied perspective. The common functional, structural, mechanistic and physiological properties are described. The various classes of these enzymes are introduced, with emphasis placed on catalytic mechanisms and their structural basis, which can provide important information to guide protein engineering approaches. Examples of enzymes of applied interest are included, often entailing metabolic engineering or protein engineering using common techniques.
                    - generic [ref=e554]:
                      - paragraph [ref=e557]: Table 1. List of recommended papers relevant to NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases.

                - generic [ref=e708]:
                  - heading "2. Specificity and promiscuity of oxidoreductases" [level=2] [ref=e709]
                  - generic [ref=e710]:
                    - text: "Promiscuity of enzymes can refer to two different concepts: catalytic promiscuity, and specificity. Promiscuous enzymes and enzymes with a wide substrate range are of particular interest, since even an initially low catalytic activity towards a specific substrate can be a good starting point to generate, by means of protein engineering and directed evolution approaches, an efficient catalyst suitable for a specific reaction, which does not necessarily match the physiological reaction carried out by the original enzyme"
                    - link "[54]" [ref=e711] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0270"
                      - generic [ref=e712]: "[54]"
                    - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e713]:
                    - text: Catalytic promiscuity is the ability of an enzyme to catalyze a second reaction in addition to the main reaction for which it is physiologically specialized, with a different transition state. Hydrolases, and in particular lipases, are some of enzymes where catalytic promiscuity has been most extensively studied, as they are able to catalyze a variety of different reactions other than hydrolysis of esters in non-aqueous media, such as transesterification alcoholysis or acidolysis
                    - link "[55]" [ref=e714] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0275"
                      - generic [ref=e715]: "[55]"
                    - text: . However, more recently other enzymes have been described to have catalytic promiscuity, including NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases. Certain alcohol dehydrogenases (in particular, alcohol dehydrogenases from horse liver,
                    - emphasis [ref=e716]: Rhodococcus ruber
                    - text: ","
                    - emphasis [ref=e717]: Ralstonia
                    - text: sp. and
                    - emphasis [ref=e718]: Lactobacillus kefir
                    - text: ) have been found to catalyze the conversion of oximes (specifically phenylacetaldoxyme) to the corresponding alcohol
                    - link "[56]" [ref=e719] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0280"
                      - generic [ref=e720]: "[56]"
                    - text: . In another study, an NADPH-dependent ene-reductase (Δ
                    - subscript [ref=e721]: "4"
                    - text: "-3-ketosteroid-5β-reductase, which reduces a C"
                    - img "single bond" [ref=e722]
                    - text: C double bond in Δ
                    - subscript [ref=e723]: "4"
                    - text: "-3-ketosteroids) gained the ability to reduce the carbonyl group of the substrate to a hydroxy group with a single point mutation, an activity that is usually carried out by 3α-hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase"
                    - link "[57]" [ref=e724] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0285"
                      - generic [ref=e725]: "[57]"
                    - text: . This indicates that the two enzymes probably had a common ancestor from which they diverged to catalyze consecutive steps of the same metabolic pathway. Substrate promiscuity refers to the property of an enzyme of being able to catalyze the same reaction with a range of different but related substrates
                    - link "[55]" [ref=e726] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0275"
                      - generic [ref=e727]: "[55]"
                    - text: . Some are very specific and have just one possible substrate, such as d-hydroxyisovalerate dehydrogenase from the depsipeptide-producing fungus
                    - emphasis [ref=e728]: Fusarium sambucinum
                    - link "[58]" [ref=e729] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0290"
                      - generic [ref=e730]: "[58]"
                    - text: . However, many NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases are not strictly specific for a single substrate, and instead display a varying degree of promiscuity, being able to act on a set of substrates. The extent to which they are promiscuous differs even between enzymes of the same subclass. For example,
                    - emphasis [ref=e731]: Thermus
                    - text: sp. ATN1 alcohol dehydrogenase (TADH) exhibits activity towards a very large range of different alcohols with multiple different functional groups (including both aliphatic and aromatic alcohols)
                    - link "[59]" [ref=e732] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0295"
                      - generic [ref=e733]: "[59]"
                    - text: ", while"
                    - emphasis [ref=e734]: Zymomonas mobilis
                    - text: alcohol dehydrogenase 2 is only able to oxidize ethanol, 1-propanol and allyl alcohol
                    - link "[60]" [ref=e735] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0300"
                      - generic [ref=e736]: "[60]"
                    - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e737]:
                    - text: There is great variation in the promiscuity of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases for the nicotinamide cofactor. Some are highly specific for either NADH/NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e738]: +
                    - text: or NADPH/NADP
                    - superscript [ref=e739]: +
                    - text: ", while others can use both with varying degrees of preference for one over the other. For example, the previously mentioned D-hydroxyisovalerate dehydrogenase of"
                    - emphasis [ref=e740]: Fusarium sambucinum
                    - text: ", as well as the soluble NADPH-dependent Fe(III) reductase from"
                    - emphasis [ref=e741]: Geobacter sulfurreducens
                    - text: ", are highly specific for NADPH/NADP"
                    - superscript [ref=e742]: +
                    - text: and are unable to accept NADH/NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e743]: +
                    - link "[58]" [ref=e744] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0290"
                      - generic [ref=e745]: "[58]"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "[61]" [ref=e746] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0305"
                      - generic [ref=e747]: "[61]"
                    - text: . At the opposite extreme, the
                    - emphasis [ref=e748]: E
                    - text: .
                    - emphasis [ref=e749]: coli
                    - text: aldo-keto reductase encoded by the
                    - emphasis [ref=e750]: ydjG gene
                    - text: is highly specific for NADH/NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e751]: +
                    - text: and is unable to accept NADPH/NADP. This enzyme is considered to be an exception among the family of aldo-keto reductases, which typically either are specific for NADPH/NADP
                    - superscript [ref=e752]: +
                    - text: or can accept both NADH/NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e753]: +
                    - text: and NADPH/NADP
                    - superscript [ref=e754]: +
                    - link "[62]" [ref=e755] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0310"
                      - generic [ref=e756]: "[62]"
                    - text: . Many NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases are described as being able to use both NADH/NAD
                    - superscript [ref=e757]: +
                    - text: and NADPH/NADP
                    - superscript [ref=e758]: +
                    - text: (
                    - link "Fig. 1" [ref=e759] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#f0005"
                      - generic [ref=e760]: Fig. 1
                    - text: ","
                    - link "Fig. 3" [ref=e761] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#f0015"
                      - generic [ref=e762]: Fig. 3
                    - text: ). These include the B2FLR2 flavin-containing monooxygenase from
                    - emphasis [ref=e763]: Stenotrophomonas maltophilia
                    - text: ", which prefers NADH/NAD"
                    - superscript [ref=e764]: +
                    - text: ", and xylose reductase of"
                    - emphasis [ref=e765]: Neurospora crassa
                    - text: ", which prefers NADPH/NADP"
                    - superscript [ref=e766]: +
                    - link "[63]" [ref=e767] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0315"
                      - generic [ref=e768]: "[63]"
                    - text: ","
                    - link "[64]" [ref=e769] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0320"
                      - generic [ref=e770]: "[64]"
                    - text: ". There are also cases in which multiple enzymes with the same catalytic activity but different cofactor specificity are found in the same organism. This is the case in barley, which contains two nitrate reductases, regulated in different ways: one of them is specific for NADH (Nar1), while the other can use both NADH and NADPH (Nar7)"
                    - link "[65]" [ref=e771] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0325"
                      - generic [ref=e772]: "[65]"
                    - text: .
                - generic [ref=e773]:
                  - heading "3. Structural features of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases" [level=2] [ref=e774]
                  - generic [ref=e775]: The number of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases for which a structure is available is growing rapidly, allowing protein engineering of these enzymes to be tackled with a rational design or data-driven approach. However, it has also become clear that it is not possible to develop a unified approach that can be broadly applied, since the huge variety of substrates upon which this class of enzymes can act as a group is associated with a similarly large variety of structural motifs. In this section we describe the two main structural features of each class of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases, important conserved catalytic residues and a brief overview of how these features relate to reaction mechanisms.
                  - generic [ref=e776]:
                    - heading "3.1. Cofactor binding domain" [level=3] [ref=e777]
                    - generic [ref=e778]:
                      - text: One of the key structural motifs of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases is the nucleotide binding domain which allows them to recruit the NAD(P)H/NAD(P)
                      - superscript [ref=e779]: +
                      - text: redox cofactor essential for their activity. The most common fold employed by these enzymes for such purpose is the Rossmann fold, but there is a variety of other less common structural motifs which can also bind the redox cofactor, such as the TIM-barrel, the dihydroquinoate synthase-like and the FAD/NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e780]: +
                      - text: binding folds
                      - link "[66]" [ref=e781] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0330"
                        - generic [ref=e782]: "[66]"
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e783]:
                      - generic [ref=e784]:
                        - text: The Rossmann fold is a common structural motif found in many nucleotide-binding proteins, such as 3-phosphoglycerate dehydrogenase and lactate dehydrogenase
                        - link "[67]" [ref=e785] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0335"
                          - generic [ref=e786]: "[67]"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "[68]" [ref=e787] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0340"
                          - generic [ref=e788]: "[68]"
                        - text: . It is named after Michael Rossmann, one of the members of the team who first identified the fold in lactate dehydrogenase
                        - link "[69]" [ref=e789] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0345"
                          - generic [ref=e790]: "[69]"
                        - text: and later realized it was a conserved motif present in other NAD(P)H-binding enzymes
                        - link "[70]" [ref=e791] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0350"
                          - generic [ref=e792]: "[70]"
                        - text: . Its most conserved core consists of two parallel β-strands separated by an α-helix (βαβ motif) (
                        - link "Fig. 4" [ref=e793] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#f0020"
                          - generic [ref=e794]: Fig. 4
                        - text: ). A tight loop is formed between the first β-strand and the α-helix, which establishes direct contact with the cofactor. This loop contains the consensus sequence Gly-X-Gly-X-X-Gly/Ala (with X being any amino acid)
                        - link "[71]" [ref=e795] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0355"
                          - generic [ref=e796]: "[71]"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "[72]" [ref=e797] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0360"
                          - generic [ref=e798]: "[72]"
                        - text: . This glycine-rich loop is involved in binding to the pyrophosphate of dinucleotides. Computational analysis revealed that a water molecule is invariably present in a very conserved position bridging the pyrophosphate to the glycine-rich loop
                        - link "[73]" [ref=e799] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0365"
                          - generic [ref=e800]: "[73]"
                        - text: .The cofactor binding domains of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases typically contain two Rossmann folds, one interacting with the adenine moiety and the other with the nicotinamide ring.
                      - figure [ref=e801]:
                        - generic [ref=e802]:
                          - img "Fig. 4" [ref=e803]
                          - list [ref=e804]:
                            - listitem [ref=e805]:
                              - 'link "Download: Download high-res image (246KB)" [ref=e806] [cursor=pointer]':
                                - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S157096391730273X-gr4_lrg.jpg
                                - generic [ref=e808]: "Download: Download high-res image (246KB)"
                            - listitem [ref=e809]:
                              - 'link "Download: Download full-size image" [ref=e810] [cursor=pointer]':
                                - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S157096391730273X-gr4.jpg
                                - generic [ref=e812]: "Download: Download full-size image"
                        - paragraph [ref=e815]: Fig. 4. Rossmann fold structural motif. A Rossmann fold is typically composed of two beta strands connected by an alpha helix. The loop connecting the first strand with the helix is of variable length and is usually involved in interactions with the cofactor. a. Diagram of the secondary structure topology of the core Rossmann fold. b. NADH bound to a Rossmann fold.
                    - generic [ref=e816]:
                      - text: The core βαβ can be extended in some cases with additional β-strands to form a larger β-sheet with the two core β-strands. Usually, all β-strands are parallel, although there are some cases where some of the strands are antiparallel
                      - link "[74]" [ref=e817] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0370"
                        - generic [ref=e818]: "[74]"
                      - text: . The segments between the additional β-strands are variable, and can consist of additional α-helices, random coil regions or complex combinations of short helices and coiled segments.
                  - generic [ref=e819]:
                    - heading "3.2. Catalytic domain" [level=3] [ref=e820]
                    - generic [ref=e821]: The other main motif of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases is the catalytic domain, which coordinates the substrate and provides the residues essential for the redox reaction to take place. Due to the very large variety of different oxidoreductases and reactions they can catalyze, it is impossible to define common motifs for all of them (other than the frequent presence of other redox cofactors such as FAD, FMN or metallic centres). Thus, here we focus on the catalytic domains of oxidoreductases and how they are connected to the nucleotide binding domain.
                    - generic [ref=e822]:
                      - text: The catalytic domains of oxidoreductases are more variable than the coenzyme binding domains. For example, NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases of the D-stereoisomers of 2-hydroxyacids (such as D-glycerate dehydrogenase or phosphoglycerate dehydrogenase) have a catalytic domain similar to their well-defined cofactor binding domain, which belongs to the family of Rossmann folds
                      - link "[75]" [ref=e823] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0375"
                        - generic [ref=e824]: "[75]"
                      - text: . On the other hand, short-chain alcohol dehydrogenases/reductases do not have a distinct, separate catalytic domain to bind the substrate. Instead, they display a single domain consisting of a Rossmann-fold scaffold with a highly variable C-terminal extension, which acts as the substrate binding site
                      - link "[76]" [ref=e825] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0380"
                        - generic [ref=e826]: "[76]"
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e827]:
                      - text: The catalytic domains of enzymes containing Rossmann folds (most of which are oxidoreductases), can belong to seven different superfamilies of the Structural Classification of Proteins (SCOP)
                      - link "[77]" [ref=e828] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0385"
                        - generic [ref=e829]: "[77]"
                      - text: . The different catalytic domains determine the substrate specificity and the exact catalytic mechanism employed by the enzyme. NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases also differ in the way that the cofactor binding domain and the catalytic domain are connected. Four different types of connections have been found. The Rossmann domain can be connected to the N terminus of the catalytic domain or to its C terminus; the catalytic domain can be inserted in the Rossmann domain, or the Rossmann domain can be inserted in the catalytic domain
                      - link "[77]" [ref=e830] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0385"
                        - generic [ref=e831]: "[77]"
                      - text: . Interestingly within each of the seven SCOP superfamilies containing Rossmann folds, the catalytic domains of each superfamily are always connected in the same way to the Rossmann domain.
                - generic [ref=e832]:
                  - heading "4. Classes of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases and reaction mechanisms" [level=2] [ref=e833]
                  - generic [ref=e834]:
                    - generic [ref=e835]:
                      - text: In the following section, we aim to give an overview of the different types of reactions catalyzed by NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases, classified according to the EC system. NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases catalyze a very broad range of redox reactions (
                      - link "Table 2" [ref=e836] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#t0010"
                        - generic [ref=e837]: Table 2
                      - text: ), and the specific catalytic mechanism differs, even within each EC class.
                      - link "Table 3" [ref=e838] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#t0015"
                        - generic [ref=e839]: Table 3
                      - text: shows a simplified summary of the different possible paths electrons can follow during the reaction catalyzed by an NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductase. Although electron transfer can happen directly between the nicotinamide cofactor and the substrate, there are also many cases where one or more intermediary redox cofactors are involved. It should be noted that while enzymes can potentially catalyze redox reactions in both directions, in practice the direction in which the reaction takes place is determined by thermodynamics and the concentration of substrates, products and cofactors. Thus, the physiological direction of the reaction might not coincide with the one described here in all cases.
                    - paragraph [ref=e843]: Table 2. Types of reactions catalyzed by NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases. Data was extracted from BRENDA and the recommendations of the Nomenclature Committee of the IUBMB [REF BRENDA + REF EC].
                    - paragraph [ref=e848]: Table 3. Possible paths of electrons during electron transfer in reactions catalyzed by NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases. Many NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases, including alcohol dehydrogenases, transfer electrons directly between the substrate and the nicotinamide cofactor. However, many others require additional intermediate cofactors, most commonly flavins and metallic centres.
                  - generic [ref=e850]:
                    - 'heading "4.1. EC 1.1.1: alcohol dehydrogenases" [level=3] [ref=e851]'
                    - generic [ref=e852]:
                      - text: Alcohol dehydrogenases constitute one of the largest and most diverse groups of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases, and they can be classified into several subfamilies according to different types of criteria. Alcohol dehydrogenases catalyze the oxidation of alcohols to the corresponding aldehydes or ketones, coupled to the reduction of NAD(P)
                      - superscript [ref=e853]: +
                      - text: to NAD(P)H, as well as the reverse reaction. Here, we describe the three families that can be distinguished based on the size of the substrate on which they can act, with enzymes of each of the families sharing structural and mechanistic features.
                    - generic [ref=e854]:
                      - generic [ref=e855]:
                        - text: Most members of the alcohol dehydrogenase family of short-chain alcohol dehydrogenases/reductases (SDRs), such as dihydropterin dehydrogenase or 17β-hydroxysteroid dehydrogenase
                        - link "[78]" [ref=e856] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0390"
                          - generic [ref=e857]: "[78]"
                        - text: ", have a key catalytic Tyr residue whose hydroxyl group donates or accepts a proton from the substrate. An adjacent Lys residue, and the nicotinamide ring of NAD(P)"
                        - superscript [ref=e858]: +
                        - text: ", lower the pKa of the hydroxyl group of the Tyr residue to facilitate its role as a proton acceptor/donor ("
                        - link "Fig. 5" [ref=e859] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#f0025"
                          - generic [ref=e860]: Fig. 5
                        - text: ). As the Tyr residue abstracts a proton from the substrate, a hydride is transferred from the substrate to the oxidized cofactor. However, in certain SDRs able to catalyze the reduction of enoyl-thioesters, the Tyr residue is present at the active site but does not act as a proton/donor acceptor, or is not present at all (such as in human peroxisomal enoyl-CoA reductase). Instead, protons are transferred directly to or from the solvent
                        - link "[76]" [ref=e861] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0380"
                          - generic [ref=e862]: "[76]"
                        - text: .
                      - figure [ref=e863]:

                    - generic [ref=e883]:
                      - generic [ref=e884]:
                        - text: Medium-chain alcohol dehydrogenases (MDRs) need Zn as a cofactor, and they use either a Tyr-based catalytic mechanism similar to SDRs, or alternatively a Zn-based mechanism. In the latter, the Zn
                        - superscript [ref=e885]: 2 +
                        - text: ion is coordinated, in the absence of the substrate, by a water molecule and three residues (two Cys and one His) adopting a tetrahedral geometry
                        - link "[79]" [ref=e886] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0395"
                          - generic [ref=e887]: "[79]"
                        - text: . After the alcohol substrate binds to the enzyme, it displaces the water molecule from the Zn coordination shell (
                        - link "Fig. 6" [ref=e888] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#f0030"
                          - generic [ref=e889]: Fig. 6
                        - text: ) and the alcohol substrate transfers a proton to the solvent, forming an alkoxide intermediate which is stabilized by the catalytic Zn
                        - superscript [ref=e890]: 2 +
                        - text: ion. The alkoxide ion transfers a hydride to the oxidized cofactor and collapses to an aldehyde/ketone
                        - link "[79]" [ref=e891] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0395"
                          - generic [ref=e892]: "[79]"
                        - text: .

                    - generic [ref=e914]:
                      - generic [ref=e915]:
                        - text: Finally, long-chain alcohol dehydrogenases (LDRs, such as the mannitol 2-dehydrogenase of
                        - emphasis [ref=e916]: Pseudomonas fluorescens
                        - text: ), constitute a heterogeneous group that differ in the catalytic mechanism and additional cofactors needed (Zn, Fe, both, or no metal at all)

                        - text: . Many employ a third catalytic mechanism different to those employed by SDR and MDR members, in which a Lys residue accepts a proton from the substrate, and then donates it to a solvent molecule. The capacity of the Lys residue to act as a general base is enhanced due to a hydrogen bond with an Asn residue (
                        - link "Fig. 7" [ref=e921] [cursor=pointer]:

                    - generic [ref=e945]:
                      - text: Iron-containing alcohol dehydrogenases have been less extensively studied than their Zn-containing counterparts
                      - link "[83]" [ref=e946] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0415"
                        - generic [ref=e947]: "[83]"
                      - text: . These enzymes contain a divalent Fe
                      - superscript [ref=e948]: 2 +
                      - text: ion which is typically coordinated by one Asp and three His residues arranged in a tetrahedral geometry. The catalytic mechanism of Fe-dependent alcohol dehydrogenases is based on a reduction of the pKa of the hydroxyl group of the substrate upon binding to the enzyme due to interaction with the F
                      - superscript [ref=e949]: 2 +
                      - text: ion, which facilitates the transfer of a hydride group to NAD +
                      - link "[84]" [ref=e950] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0420"

                      - text: ","
                      - link "[85]" [ref=e952] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0425"

                      - text: . Fe-dependent alcohol dehydrogenases have been reported to be sensitive to oxygen inactivation, unlike Zn-dependent enzymes, due to Fe-catalyzed oxidation reactions that can take place in the presence of oxygen
                      - link "[86]" [ref=e954] [cursor=pointer]:

                  - generic [ref=e958]:
                    - 'heading "4.2. EC 1.2.1: aldehyde dehydrogenases" [level=3] [ref=e959]'
                    - generic [ref=e960]:
                      - text: Aldehyde dehydrogenases catalyze the oxidation of aldehydes to carboxylic acids, or in some cases their CoA esters or acyl carrier protein (ACP) thioesters, coupled to the reduction of NAD(P)
                      - superscript [ref=e961]: +
                      - text: to NAD(P)H, as well as the reverse reaction. Enzymes of this class are sometimes referred to by other names, such as carboxylic acid reductases (CAR), depending upon their substrate specificity and/or the physiological direction of the reaction.
                    - generic [ref=e962]:
                      - generic [ref=e963]:
                        - text: The key catalytic residue of aldehyde dehydrogenases is a Cys residue from whose sulfhydryl group a proton is abstracted by a Glu residue, or by a His residue in the exceptional case of
                        - emphasis [ref=e964]: Vibrio harveyi
                        - text: aldehyde dehydrogenase (
                        - link "Fig. 8" [ref=e965] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#f0040"
                          - generic [ref=e966]: Fig. 8
                        - text: )
                        - link "[88]" [ref=e967] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0440"
                          - generic [ref=e968]: "[88]"
                        - text: . The thiolate can act as a nucleophile and attack the carbon of the carbonyl group. This nucleophilic attack causes the formation of a thiohemiacetal intermediate. The thiohemiacetal contains a negatively charged oxygen atom which is stabilized, at least in part, by an Asn residue which is also essential for catalysis. The thiohemiacetal oxyanion then spontaneously donates a hydride group to NAD(P)
                        - superscript [ref=e969]: +
                        - text: . In this process, a thioester bond is formed between the aldehyde and the catalytic Cys. The thioester bond is hydrolysed by a water molecule (which is activated by a Glu residue to carry out hydrolysis), allowing the carboxylic acid to be released
                        - link "[89]" [ref=e970] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0445"
                          - generic [ref=e971]: "[89]"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "[90]" [ref=e972] [cursor=pointer]:

                        - text: .
