                        - /url: "#bb0465"
                        - generic [ref=e1002]: "[93]"
                      - text: ", and have been the subject of recent metabolic engineering studies"
                      - link "[94]" [ref=e1003] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0470"
                        - generic [ref=e1004]: "[94]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[95]" [ref=e1005] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0475"
                        - generic [ref=e1006]: "[95]"
                      - text: . The CoA-dependent aldehyde dehydrogenases can occur independently
                      - link "[96]" [ref=e1007] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0480"
                        - generic [ref=e1008]: "[96]"
                      - text: or as part of bifunctional aldehyde-alcohol dehydrogenases (ADHE) which are present in many bacteria
                      - link "[91]" [ref=e1009] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0455"
                        - generic [ref=e1010]: "[91]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[92]" [ref=e1011] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0460"
                        - generic [ref=e1012]: "[92]"
                      - text: . Bifunctional aldehyde-alcohol dehydrogenases are able to catalyze the sequential reduction of a CoA ester to the aldehyde and then to the alcohol, most commonly acetyl-CoA to acetaldehyde and then to ethanol. They typically comprise two main domains, an N-terminal aldehyde dehydrogenase domain and an iron-dependent alcohol dehydrogenase C-terminal domain. The two domains are connected by a small linker
                      - link "[97]" [ref=e1013] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0485"
                        - generic [ref=e1014]: "[97]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[98]" [ref=e1015] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0490"
                        - generic [ref=e1016]: "[98]"
                      - text: . It is unclear whether CoA-dependent aldehyde dehydrogenases gained CoA ester formation activity during evolution, or if this feature was originally present in primitive aldehyde dehydrogenases but it was later lost in most of them
                      - link "[99]" [ref=e1017] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0495"
                        - generic [ref=e1018]: "[99]"
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1019]:
                      - text: Carboxylic acid reductases are able to reduce fatty acids (carboxylic acids with a long aliphatic chain) to the corresponding aldehydes are of special interest due to their ability to generate alcohols and alkanes from the carboxylic acids naturally found in oils and fats. An NADPH-dependent oxidoreductase able to catalyze the reduction of a wide range of fatty acids (with an aliphatic chain containing between 6 and 18C atoms) has been identified in
                      - emphasis [ref=e1020]: Mycobacterium marinum
                      - text: ", with the peculiarity over other aldehyde dehydrogenases that the reaction requires ATP in addition to NADPH"
                      - link "[100]" [ref=e1021] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0500"
                        - generic [ref=e1022]: "[100]"
                      - text: . An
                      - emphasis [ref=e1023]: E
                      - text: .
                      - emphasis [ref=e1024]: coli
                      - text: strain able to convert fatty acids into long-chain alcohols and alkanes (which have numerous applications as fuels, detergents, food additives and many others) was engineered by introducing the gene encoding the CAR from
                      - emphasis [ref=e1025]: M
                      - text: .
                      - emphasis [ref=e1026]: marinum
                      - text: along with an aldehyde reductase (a synonym for alcohol dehydrogenase) to form alcohols and an aldehyde decarbonylase, a recently-discovered enzyme able to catalyze the conversion of a long-chain aldehyde to an alkane and formate
                      - link "[100]" [ref=e1027] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0500"
                        - generic [ref=e1028]: "[100]"
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1029]:
                      - text: Another type of aldehyde dehydrogenase, known as fatty acyl-CoA/ACP reductase, has also been investigated for the ability to produce long-chain aldehydes and alcohols. These enzymes are able to form long-chain aldehydes by reduction of fatty acids bound to acyl carrier protein (ACP) and/or to CoA, the latter case being similar to the CoA-dependent aldehyde dehydrogenases described above, but acting on long-chain substrates. Some of the enzymes of this group prefer acyl-CoA as the substrate, while others act more efficiently on acyl-ACP substrates, so will generate products from CoA-dependent fatty acid beta-oxidation or ACP-dependent fatty acid synthesis pathways, respectively, both of which are being investigated through metabolic engineering
                      - link "[101]" [ref=e1030] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0505"
                        - generic [ref=e1031]: "[101]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[102]" [ref=e1032] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0510"
                        - generic [ref=e1033]: "[102]"
                      - text: . Interestingly, some of them are able to reduce fatty acid acyl-CoA/ACP directly to long-chain alcohols, requiring oxidation of two molecules of NAD(P)H, instead of one
                      - link "[103]" [ref=e1034] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0515"
                        - generic [ref=e1035]: "[103]"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e1036]:
                    - 'heading "4.3. EC 1.3.1: ene reductases" [level=3] [ref=e1037]'
                    - generic [ref=e1038]:
                      - text: Ene reductases catalyze the hydrogenation of activated alkenes to the corresponding alkanes. Only activated alkenes are substrates for these enzymes. For example, one of the carbons participating in the double bond should have an electron-withdrawing group (EWG) as a substituent (a functional group able to remove electron density from the double bond, making it more electrophilic). The carbon with the EWG is referred to as Cα, while the other carbon atom is called Cβ. The reaction starts with the transfer of a hydride group from a reduced flavin cofactor to the Cβ, followed by the addition of a proton from a nearby Tyr residue to Cα. The proton donated by the Tyr residue is replenished by a water molecule. Finally, a reduced NAD(P)H molecule reduces the oxidized flavin cofactor
                      - link "[104]" [ref=e1039] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0520"
                        - generic [ref=e1040]: "[104]"
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1041]:
                      - text: One of the largest groups of ene reductases are the members of the long-known family of Old Yellow Enzymes (OYE). OYE was first identified in the 1930s as a yellow enzyme able to form a complete respiratory system together with glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase acting on glucose-6-phosphate and using molecular oxygen as the final electron acceptor
                      - link "[105]" [ref=e1042] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0525"
                        - generic [ref=e1043]: "[105]"
                      - text: . Since then, variants have been identified in yeasts, plants and bacteria
                      - link "[106]" [ref=e1044] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0530"
                        - generic [ref=e1045]: "[106]"
                      - text: . When it was purified by Theorell in 1935, it was shown to contain a protein, colourless component and a yellow component essential for activity
                      - link "[107]" [ref=e1046] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0535"
                        - generic [ref=e1047]: "[107]"
                      - text: . Theorell later demonstrated that the yellow component was riboflavin 5′-phosphate, also known as flavin mononucleotide (FMN)
                      - link "[108]" [ref=e1048] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0540"
                        - generic [ref=e1049]: "[108]"
                      - text: . Extensive biochemical and structural studies have been carried out with OYEs, which have demonstrated their ability to reduce a large variety of unsaturated compounds to their saturated counterparts. However, their physiological function has not been completely established, although proposals suggesting a conserved role in the detoxification of electrophilic compounds have been made
                      - link "[106]" [ref=e1050] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0530"
                        - generic [ref=e1051]: "[106]"
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1052]:
                      - text: Many of the reactions catalyzed by OYEs are interesting from the perspective of an industrial application. For example, OYE can reduce nitroalkenes to nitroalkanes in a process involving the formation of a nitronate intermediate. OYE1 from
                      - emphasis [ref=e1053]: Saccharomyces carlsbergensis
                      - text: with Y196F mutation has been shown to cause the accumulation of the nitronate intermediate, which could be then chemically alkylated to generate nitroalkanes with two chiral centers
                      - link "[106]" [ref=e1054] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0530"
                        - generic [ref=e1055]: "[106]"
                      - text: . Another potential application is to use them to easily carry out small modifications in the structure of drugs to alter their pharmaceutical properties, such as morphine alkaloids. Coexpressing a morphinone reductase (an OYE) with a morphine dehydrogenase in
                      - emphasis [ref=e1056]: E
                      - text: .
                      - emphasis [ref=e1057]: coli
                      - text: results in a strain able to efficiently convert morphine and codeine to hydromorphone and hydrocodone, which are effective analgesics
                      - link "[109]" [ref=e1058] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0545"
                        - generic [ref=e1059]: "[109]"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e1060]:
                    - 'heading "4.4. EC 1.4.1: amino acid dehydrogenases" [level=3] [ref=e1061]'
                    - generic [ref=e1062]:
                      - text: The general reaction catalyzed by amino acid dehydrogenases is the transfer of hydride from the Cα atom of an amino acid to NAD(P)
                      - superscript [ref=e1063]: +
                      - text: . As a consequence, the corresponding α-keto acid and ammonium are generated. Two amino acid dehydrogenases whose catalytic mechanism has been well studied are glutamate dehydrogenase and phenylalanine dehydrogenase
                      - link "[110]" [ref=e1064] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0550"
                        - generic [ref=e1065]: "[110]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[111]" [ref=e1066] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0555"
                        - generic [ref=e1067]: "[111]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[112]" [ref=e1068] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0560"
                        - generic [ref=e1069]: "[112]"
                      - text: . In both cases, the catalytic mechanism is very similar. Initially, an Asp residue acts a general base and abstracts a proton from the α-amino group of the amino acid. Then, a hydride ion is transferred to NAD(P)
                      - superscript [ref=e1070]: +
                      - text: from the Cα, forming an intermediate α-imino acid
                      - link "[111]" [ref=e1071] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0555"
                        - generic [ref=e1072]: "[111]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[113]" [ref=e1073] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0565"
                        - generic [ref=e1074]: "[113]"
                      - text: . Finally, the intermediate imino acid is hydrolyzed to the corresponding α-keto acid and ammonium
                      - link "[111]" [ref=e1075] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0555"
                        - generic [ref=e1076]: "[111]"
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1077]:
                      - text: Amino acid dehydrogenases play a key role in the catabolism of amino acids, since they allow the removal of their amino group to take place, which is a necessary step for their incorporation into other catabolic pathways, such as the tricarboxylic acid cycle (TCA cycle). In the deamination process, an ATP molecule is generated (which can be used for energy-requiring processes), and the amino group is released as free ammonia, which can then be employed by the cell for different purposes. Additionally, some organisms use the reverse reaction catalyzed by amino acid dehydrogenases to incorporate free ammonia into carbon skeletons when the levels of free ammonia are high
                      - link "[20]" [ref=e1078] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0100"
                        - generic [ref=e1079]: "[20]"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e1080]:
                    - 'heading "4.5. EC 1.5.1: oxidoreductases of CHsingle bondNH groups" [level=3] [ref=e1081]':
                      - text: "4.5. EC 1.5.1: oxidoreductases of CH"
                      - img "single bond" [ref=e1082]
                      - text: NH groups
                    - generic [ref=e1083]:
                      - text: This family of oxidoreductases comprises a group of enzymatic activities acting on a rather large variety of substrates, with the only common feature being that all of them contain a single or double C
                      - img "single bond" [ref=e1084]
                      - text: N bond which donates electrons to reduce NAD(P)
                      - superscript [ref=e1085]: +
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1086]:
                      - text: Dihydropteridine reductase (EC 1.5.1.34), a key enzyme for the synthesis of folate, catalyzes the reduction of a quinonoid 6,7-dihydropteridine to the corresponding 5,6,7,8-tetrahydropteridine. NAD(P)H provides the required electrons, which are accepted by the pyrazine heteroaromatic ring. The catalytic mechanism is very similar to that of the SDR subfamily of alcohol dehydrogenases, with a Tyr residue directly participating in proton transfer from or to the substrate and a Lys residue facilitating the role of the Tyr residue by lowering the pKa of the hydroxyl group of the Tyr residue
                      - link "[21]" [ref=e1087] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0105"
                        - generic [ref=e1088]: "[21]"
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1089]:
                      - generic [ref=e1090]:
                        - text: Dihydrofolate reductase (EC 1.5.1.3), which is also essential for the synthesis of folate, is another example of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductase acting on CH-NH groups. However, in this case an Asp residue plays the central role in catalysis (
                        - link "Fig. 9" [ref=e1091] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#f0045"
                          - generic [ref=e1092]: Fig. 9
                        - text: ). The Asp residue forms a hydrogen bond with the N3 atom of the pterin ring of the substrate and a water molecule which establishes another hydrogen bond with the O5 atom. The pKa of the N5 atom of the pterin ring is thus substantially elevated, making it able to accept a proton directly from a water molecule. Finally, after the proton has been accepted by the substrate, hydride transfer from the reduced cofactor takes place
                        - link "[114]" [ref=e1093] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0570"
                          - generic [ref=e1094]: "[114]"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "[115]" [ref=e1095] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0575"
                          - generic [ref=e1096]: "[115]"
                        - text: .
                      - figure [ref=e1097]:
                        - generic [ref=e1098]:
                          - img "Fig. 9"
                          - list [ref=e1099]:
                            - listitem [ref=e1100]:
                              - 'link "Download: Download high-res image (231KB)" [ref=e1101] [cursor=pointer]':
                                - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S157096391730273X-gr9_lrg.jpg
                                - generic [ref=e1103]: "Download: Download high-res image (231KB)"
                            - listitem [ref=e1104]:
                              - 'link "Download: Download full-size image" [ref=e1105] [cursor=pointer]':
                                - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S157096391730273X-gr9.jpg
                                - generic [ref=e1107]: "Download: Download full-size image"
                        - paragraph [ref=e1110]:
                          - text: Fig. 9. Active site of
                          - emphasis [ref=e1111]: E
                          - text: .
                          - emphasis [ref=e1112]: coli
                          - text: dihydrofolate reductase (PDB code
                          - link "7DFROpens in new window" [ref=e1113] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: http://www.rcsb.org/pdb/explore.do?structureId=7DFR
                            - generic [ref=e1114]:
                              - text: 7DFR
                              - img "Opens in new window" [ref=e1115]
                          - text: ). Substrate is displayed in purple, NADP
                          - superscript [ref=e1117]: +
                          - text: is displayed in grey with heteroatoms coloured independently and residues that are essential for enzyme activity are labelled and coloured in dark grey.
                    - generic [ref=e1118]:
                      - text: Dihydrofolate reductase is particularly interesting, as it is the target of different types of drugs. For example, methotrexate is an inhibitor of dihydrofolate reductase (DHFR) used as a chemotherapy agent for several types of cancer. The use of a DHFR inhibitor takes advantage of the fact that rapidly dividing cells have a very high demand for folate since it is necessary to synthesize thymine. Some antibacterial agents aim to specifically inhibit bacterial DHFR, such as trimethoprim. Trimethoprim is used in the standard therapy against the opportunistic pathogen
                      - emphasis [ref=e1119]: Pneumocystis jirovecii
                      - text: . In a recent study, six variants with a single amino acid substitution of
                      - emphasis [ref=e1120]: Pneumocystis jirovecii
                      - text: DHFR were found to confer resistance against trimethoprim. An experimental drug also targeting DHFR but with a higher degree of conformational flexibility than trimethoprim (known as OAAG324) was tested against these variants, and found to be effective with one of them
                      - link "[116]" [ref=e1121] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0580"
                        - generic [ref=e1122]: "[116]"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e1123]:
                    - 'heading "4.6. EC 1.6.1: NAD(P)+ transhydrogenases" [level=3] [ref=e1124]':
                      - text: "4.6. EC 1.6.1: NAD(P)"
                      - superscript [ref=e1125]: +
                      - text: transhydrogenases
                    - generic [ref=e1126]:
                      - text: Pyridine nucleotide transhydrogenases catalyze the reversible transfer of hydride between NADH/NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1127]: +
                      - text: and NADPH/NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e1128]: +
                      - text: "pools. In most cases, the reaction is as follows: NADPH + NAD"
                      - superscript [ref=e1129]: +
                      - text: ⇌ NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e1130]: +
                      - text: "+ NADH. The hydrogen atom is transferred from the C4 carbon atom of the reduced cofactor to the C4 carbon atom of the oxidized cofactor. Two groups of transhydrogenases can be distinguished: energy-linked and non-energy-linked transhydrogenases."
                    - generic [ref=e1131]:
                      - text: Energy-linked transhydrogenases are integral membrane proteins which do not require any flavin cofactor. They receive this name because they couple the transhydrogenation reaction to proton translocation across the membrane where they are located. They are found in mitochondria, some heterotrophic bacteria and photosynthesizing bacteria
                      - link "[117]" [ref=e1132] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0585"
                        - generic [ref=e1133]: "[117]"
                      - text: . Thanks to the proton gradient generated across both the mitochondrial inner membrane and the cell membrane of bacteria, the apparent equilibrium constant of the system is changed, causing a shift towards the production of NADPH and NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1134]: +
                      - link "[118]" [ref=e1135] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0590"
                        - generic [ref=e1136]: "[118]"
                      - text: . In eukaryotes, energy-linked transhydrogenase has been suggested to act as a redox buffer able to counteract both an excessive depletion of NADPH and a dissipation of the proton gradient. In prokaryotes, the physiological role is believed to be mostly the generation of NADPH for anabolism
                      - link "[4]" [ref=e1137] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0020"
                        - generic [ref=e1138]: "[4]"
                      - text: . These transhydrogenases are also called
                      - emphasis [ref=e1139]: Re
                      - text: /
                      - emphasis [ref=e1140]: Si
                      - text: "-specific transhydrogenases or AB transhydrogenases, as they transfer hydrogen 4A of the nicotinamide ring (situated on its"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1141]: Re
                      - text: face) from NADH to NADP +, or hydrogen 4B (situated on the
                      - emphasis [ref=e1142]: Si
                      - text: face) from NADPH to NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1143]: +
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1144]:
                      - text: Non energy-linked transhydrogenases are soluble flavoproteins which contain FAD. They are less widespread than energy-linked transhydrogenases and only found in some heterotrophic bacteria. Usually, only one of the two types of transhydrogenases is found in a specific organism, with the exception of
                      - emphasis [ref=e1145]: Enterobacteriaceae
                      - text: (including
                      - emphasis [ref=e1146]: Escherichia coli
                      - text: ), which possess one energy-linked and one non energy-linked transhydrogenase encoded by the
                      - emphasis [ref=e1147]: pntAB
                      - text: and
                      - emphasis [ref=e1148]: udhA
                      - text: genes respectively. Non energy-linked transhydrogenases are also known as
                      - emphasis [ref=e1149]: Si
                      - text: /
                      - emphasis [ref=e1150]: Si
                      - text: "-specific transhydrogenases or BB transhydrogenases, since they transfer hydrogen 4B of the nicotinamide ring, both from NADH to NADP"
                      - superscript [ref=e1151]: +
                      - text: and from NADPH to NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1152]: +
                      - text: . Contrary to energy-linked transhydrogenases, it has been suggested that the physiological role of non-energy-linked transhydrogenases is to convert NADPH to NADH
                      - link "[4]" [ref=e1153] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0020"
                        - generic [ref=e1154]: "[4]"
                      - text: . However, this seems to be dependent on growth conditions and organism, with some reports indicating that non energy-linked transhydrogenases can actually increase NADPH availability when the demand for NADPH is increased
                      - link "[119]" [ref=e1155] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0595"
                        - generic [ref=e1156]: "[119]"
                      - text: . Interestingly, it has been found that expressing a non-energy-linked transhydrogenase in
                      - emphasis [ref=e1157]: Synechococcus
                      - text: results in a reduced growth rate
                      - link "[120]" [ref=e1158] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0600"
                        - generic [ref=e1159]: "[120]"
                      - text: ", perhaps reflecting the importance of the highly reduced state of the NADPH pool normally maintained in cyanobacteria."
                    - generic [ref=e1160]:
                      - text: Both types of transhydrogenases have been explored as metabolic engineering targets to improve the yield of products whose synthesis depends on cofactor availability. Results have been mixed, with some studies showing that overexpressing
                      - emphasis [ref=e1161]: pntAB
                      - text: is sufficient to enhance NADPH regeneration
                      - link "[121]" [ref=e1162] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0605"
                        - generic [ref=e1163]: "[121]"
                      - text: while others show that the overexpression of
                      - emphasis [ref=e1164]: pntAB
                      - text: does not increase NADPH availability and can even decrease it
                      - link "[122]" [ref=e1165] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0610"
                        - generic [ref=e1166]: "[122]"
                      - text: . In the latter study, it was found that overexpressing
                      - emphasis [ref=e1167]: udhA
                      - text: increased the levels of available NADPH.
                  - generic [ref=e1168]:
                    - 'heading "4.7. EC 1.7.1: oxidoreductases acting on other nitrogenous compounds (other than CHsingle bondNH groups)" [level=3] [ref=e1169]':
                      - text: "4.7. EC 1.7.1: oxidoreductases acting on other nitrogenous compounds (other than CH"
                      - img "single bond" [ref=e1170]
                      - text: NH groups)
                    - generic [ref=e1171]:
                      - text: This group includes dehydrogenases acting on a variety of different compounds containing nitrogen and which cannot be classified into any of the other categories of oxidoreductases. Substrates include both inorganic compounds (such as nitrate and nitrite) and organic compounds (such as azobenzene and nitroquinoline). The exact reaction mechanisms of many of these enzymes are still poorly understood, partly due to the lack of structural information for many of them, although for an example see
                      - link "[123]" [ref=e1172] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0615"
                        - generic [ref=e1173]: "[123]"
                      - text: . However, in most of the cases NAD(P)H does not directly transfer electrons to the substrate, but instead provides the electrons to regenerate the reduced form of the other cofactor which directly participates in the reduction of the substrate.
                    - generic [ref=e1174]:
                      - text: "NAD(P)H-dependent nitrate reductases (EC 1.7.1.1–3) are relatively well characterized, and they catalyze the reduction of nitrate to nitrite, with NAD(P)H providing the source of electrons for the reduction of nitrogen. There are four different types of nitrate reductases (NRs): eukaryotic assimilatory NR, cytoplasmic bacterial assimilatory NR, membrane-bound bacterial respiratory NR and periplasmic bacterial dissimilatory NR"
                      - link "[124]" [ref=e1175] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0620"
                        - generic [ref=e1176]: "[124]"
                      - text: . Only assimilatory nitrate reductases, which carry out the incorporation of nitrogen into organic molecules for growth, are directly NAD(P)H-dependent (nitrate and periplasmic nitrate reductases). All NRs contain a molybdenum cofactor (Mo-Co) with a molybdenum atom bound to a set of ligands which varies across the different NRs. Eukaryotic nitrate reductases contain molybdopterin, while in prokaryotic enzymes the Mo-Co is molybdopterine-guanine dinucleotide.
                    - generic [ref=e1177]:
                      - text: Eukaryotic NRs have been extensively studied, and their reaction mechanism is well known due to the available structural information. In eukaryotic NRs, Mo is initially in its Mo
                      - superscript [ref=e1178]: 4 +
                      - text: state. When nitrate binds to the active site, it forms the reaction intermediate by coordinating the Mo atom with one of its O atoms, displacing a water molecule which was previously bound to Mo. The formation of the reaction intermediate causes Mo electrons to shift towards the created Mo
                      - img "single bond" [ref=e1179]
                      - text: O bond. As a consequence, Mo is oxidized to Mo
                      - superscript [ref=e1180]: 6 +
                      - text: and the bond between Mo and the coordinating O atom from nitrate becomes a double bond. This causes the bond between this O atom and nitrate to break, forming nitrite which is subsequently released. Finally, transfer of two electrons and two protons from NAD(P)H
                      - emphasis [ref=e1181]: via
                      - text: a cytochrome
                      - emphasis [ref=e1182]: b
                      - text: 5 domain cause regeneration of Mo
                      - superscript [ref=e1183]: 4 +
                      - text: and reduction of the O atom bound by a double bond to a water ligand
                      - link "[125]" [ref=e1184] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0625"
                        - generic [ref=e1185]: "[125]"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e1186]:
                    - 'heading "4.8. EC 1.8.1: oxidoreductases acting on a sulfur group" [level=3] [ref=e1187]'
                    - generic [ref=e1188]: This group also consists of a diverse set of oxidoreductases whose only common characteristic is that they act on a sulfur-containing group of their substrate, which can be of very different types. These include both inorganic forms of sulfur (such as sulfite and sulfide), and a variety of sulfur-containing organic molecules (including cysteine, glutathione, coenzyme A and many others). As in the case of enzymes belonging to EC 1.7.1, the role of NAD(P)H is to provide electrons to regenerate the other cofactors directly involved in the catalysis.
                    - generic [ref=e1189]:
                      - text: NADPH-dependent sulfite reductase (EC 1.8.1.2), such as
                      - emphasis [ref=e1190]: E
                      - text: .
                      - emphasis [ref=e1191]: coli
                      - text: sulfite reductase, catalyzes the reduction of sulfite to sulfide by transferring electrons provided by NADPH, one of the key steps of the assimilation of inorganic sulfur into organic molecules. This enzyme shares structural and mechanistic features with nitrite reductase, including the presence of a sirohaem cofactor and a [4Fe-4S] cluster. Additionally, both of them catalyze the transfer of six electrons (which leads to the formation of ammonia in the case of nitrite reductase). In fact, purified sulfite reductase is also able to catalyze the reduction of nitrite to ammonia
                      - link "[126]" [ref=e1192] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0630"
                        - generic [ref=e1193]: "[126]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[127]" [ref=e1194] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0635"
                        - generic [ref=e1195]: "[127]"
                      - text: . The reaction mechanism involves three successive transfers of two electrons, provided by NADPH, and protons to the oxygen atoms of sulfite, which binds as an axial ligand of the sirohaem Fe atom
                      - link "[29]" [ref=e1196] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0145"
                        - generic [ref=e1197]: "[29]"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e1198]:
                    - 'heading "4.9. EC 1.10.1: dehydrogenases of diphenols" [level=3] [ref=e1199]'
                    - generic [ref=e1200]:
                      - text: "EC class 1.10 includes all oxidoreductases able to act on diphenols and related products. There are five subtypes this EC class, which vary in the redox partner they use to transfer the electrons from the diphenol substrate: NAD(P)"
                      - superscript [ref=e1201]: +
                      - text: ", a cytochrome, oxygen, a quinone, or a copper protein. Those using oxygen as the electron acceptor comprise by far the highest diversity of enzymatic activities, while there is only one known enzymatic activity of such type dependent on NAD(P)"
                      - superscript [ref=e1202]: +
                      - text: ":"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1203]: trans
                      - text: "-acenaphthene-1,2-diol dehydrogenase (EC 1.10.1.1), which oxidizes said substrate to acenaphthenequinone. This NAD(P)H-dependent enzyme was identified and purified in 1973 from rat (and other mammalian) liver extracts"
                      - link "[32]" [ref=e1204] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0160"
                        - generic [ref=e1205]: "[32]"
                      - text: . However, the structure of the enzyme has not been solved, and no information about the reaction mechanism is available.
                  - generic [ref=e1206]:
                    - 'heading "4.10. EC 1.11.1: peroxidases" [level=3] [ref=e1207]'
                    - generic [ref=e1208]:
                      - text: Peroxidases catalyze the transfer of electrons from an electron donor to H
                      - subscript [ref=e1209]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e1210]: "2"
                      - text: . Both NADH-dependent and NADPH-dependent peroxidases have been found (EC 1.11.1.1 and EC 1.11.1.2 respectively). The reaction they catalyze is NAD(P)H + H
                      - superscript [ref=e1211]: +
                      - text: + H
                      - subscript [ref=e1212]: "2"
                      - text: O
                      - subscript [ref=e1213]: "2"
                      - text: ⇌ NAD(P)
                      - superscript [ref=e1214]: +
                      - text: + 2 H
                      - subscript [ref=e1215]: "2"
                      - text: O.
                    - generic [ref=e1216]:
                      - generic [ref=e1217]:
                        - text: NADH peroxidase has been well characterized, and its structure and mechanism long known. In addition to NADH, the catalytic centre of the enzyme includes FAD, a Cys residue and a His residue (
                        - link "Fig. 10" [ref=e1218] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#f0050"
                          - generic [ref=e1219]: Fig. 10
                        - text: ). The Cys residue is adjacent to the FAD cofactor. As a first step, NADH binds to the enzyme with FAD in its oxidized state. The binding of NADH causes the electron density of the isoalloxazine group of FAD to shift towards the Cys residue. Then, H
                        - subscript [ref=e1220]: "2"
                        - text: O
                        - subscript [ref=e1221]: "2"
                        - text: binds to the enzyme forming hydrogen bonds with the Cys and His residues, and other amino acids close to the active site. The Cys residue performs a nucleophilic attack on H
                        - subscript [ref=e1222]: "2"
                        - text: O
                        - subscript [ref=e1223]: "2"
                        - text: ", cleaving the O"
                        - img "single bond" [ref=e1224]
                        - text: O bond to yield an OH
                        - superscript [ref=e1225]: −
                        - text: anion and a sulfenic acid derivative. The hydroxide anion abstracts a proton from the His residue, and is released as a water molecule. The sulfenic acid derivative is reduced by the transfer of two electrons and a proton from NADH through FAD, leading to the formation of a second water molecule which is released together with NAD
                        - superscript [ref=e1226]: +
                        - text: . Finally, the His residue receives a proton from the solvent, to regenerate its initial protonated state
                        - link "[33]" [ref=e1227] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0165"
                          - generic [ref=e1228]: "[33]"
                        - text: .
                      - figure [ref=e1229]:
                        - generic [ref=e1230]:
                          - img "Fig. 10"
                          - list [ref=e1231]:
                            - listitem [ref=e1232]:
                              - 'link "Download: Download high-res image (281KB)" [ref=e1233] [cursor=pointer]':
                                - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S157096391730273X-gr10_lrg.jpg
                                - generic [ref=e1235]: "Download: Download high-res image (281KB)"
                            - listitem [ref=e1236]:
                              - 'link "Download: Download full-size image" [ref=e1237] [cursor=pointer]':
                                - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S157096391730273X-gr10.jpg
                                - generic [ref=e1239]: "Download: Download full-size image"
                        - paragraph [ref=e1242]:
                          - text: Fig. 10. Active site of
                          - emphasis [ref=e1243]: Enterococcus faecalis
                          - text: NADH-dependent peroxidase (PDB code
                          - link "2NPXOpens in new window" [ref=e1244] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: http://www.rcsb.org/pdb/explore.do?structureId=2NPX
                            - generic [ref=e1245]:
                              - text: 2NPX
                              - img "Opens in new window" [ref=e1246]
                          - text: ). NAD
                          - superscript [ref=e1248]: +
                          - text: and FAD are displayed in grey and light blue respectively with heteroatoms coloured independently and residues that are essential for enzyme activity are labelled and coloured in dark grey.
                    - generic [ref=e1249]:
                      - text: Another class of NAD(P)H dependent peroxidases catalyzes the reduction of hydroperoxy derivatives of fatty acids to the corresponding hydroxy acids (EC 1.11.1.22). The enzyme was originally found in
                      - emphasis [ref=e1250]: Synechocystis
                      - text: PCC 6803
                      - link "[128]" [ref=e1251] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0640"
                        - generic [ref=e1252]: "[128]"
                      - text: ", and uses electrons from NADPH to reduce peroxidized lipids to hydroxy acids. These enzymes share sequence similarity to glutathione peroxidases, but no catalytic activity can be detected when reduced glutathione is provided as the electron donor. One of the major differences with glutathione peroxidases is the absence of a selenocysteine residue, which is replaced by a standard Cys residue. This type of NADPH-dependent peroxidases has been hypothesized to serve as a defense system against hydroperoxide derivatives of unsaturated fatty acids, which can lead to oxidative damage"
                      - link "[128]" [ref=e1253] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0640"
                        - generic [ref=e1254]: "[128]"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e1255]:
                    - 'heading "4.11. EC 1.12.1: oxidoreductases of H2 (hydrogenases)" [level=3] [ref=e1256]':
                      - text: "4.11. EC 1.12.1: oxidoreductases of H"
                      - subscript [ref=e1257]: "2"
                      - text: (hydrogenases)
                    - generic [ref=e1258]:
                      - text: "Hydrogenases catalyze the oxidation of molecular hydrogen or reduction of protons and are classified into three main phylogenetically distinct classes, based on the metals found at their active site: [NiFe]-hydrogenases, [FeFe]-hydrogenases, and the lesser-studied [Fe-only]-hydrogenases. In all cases, the catalytic mechanism of hydrogen oxidation or proton reduction requires electron transfer to and from the active-site metals, which are directly responsible for the oxidation of hydrogen or reduction of protons. The electrons can be transferred to or from a variety of donors/acceptors, which can include NAD(P)H/NAD(P)"
                      - superscript [ref=e1259]: +
                      - text: . In all cases, the transfer of electrons occurs
                      - emphasis [ref=e1260]: via
                      - text: an iron sulfur (Fe-S) cluster relay.
                    - generic [ref=e1261]:
                      - generic [ref=e1262]:
                        - text: The active site of [NiFe]-hydrogenases is ligated by four Cys residues. Two of the Cys are bridging ligands of both metal ions, while the other two are bound to the Ni atom alone
                        - link "[129]" [ref=e1263] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0645"
                          - generic [ref=e1264]: "[129]"
                        - text: . The Fe atom binds three diatomic ligands
                        - link "[130]" [ref=e1265] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0650"
                          - generic [ref=e1266]: "[130]"
                        - text: ", two CN"
                        - superscript [ref=e1267]: −
                        - text: ligands and one CO ligand
                        - link "[131]" [ref=e1268] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0655"
                          - generic [ref=e1269]: "[131]"
                        - text: (
                        - link "Fig. 11" [ref=e1270] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#f0055"
                          - generic [ref=e1271]: Fig. 11
                        - text: ). The bridging ligand between the metal ions is either a hydroxo or a hydro-peroxide depending on the redox state of the active site
                        - link "[132]" [ref=e1272] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0660"
                          - generic [ref=e1273]: "[132]"
                        - text: .
                      - figure [ref=e1274]:
                        - generic [ref=e1275]:
                          - img "Fig. 11"
                          - list [ref=e1276]:
                            - listitem [ref=e1277]:
                              - 'link "Download: Download high-res image (212KB)" [ref=e1278] [cursor=pointer]':
                                - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S157096391730273X-gr11_lrg.jpg
                                - generic [ref=e1280]: "Download: Download high-res image (212KB)"
                            - listitem [ref=e1281]:
                              - 'link "Download: Download full-size image" [ref=e1282] [cursor=pointer]':
                                - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S157096391730273X-gr11.jpg
                                - generic [ref=e1284]: "Download: Download full-size image"
                        - paragraph [ref=e1287]:
                          - text: Fig. 11. Active site of
                          - emphasis [ref=e1288]: Hydrogenophilus thermoluteolus
                          - text: "[NiFe]-hydrogenase (PDB code"
                          - link "5XF9Opens in new window" [ref=e1289] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: http://www.rcsb.org/pdb/explore.do?structureId=5XF9
                            - generic [ref=e1290]:
                              - text: 5XF9
                              - img "Opens in new window" [ref=e1291]
                          - text: ). Ni
                          - superscript [ref=e1293]: 3 +
                          - text: is displayed in green and Fe
                          - superscript [ref=e1294]: 2 +
                          - text: is displayed in orange. Ligands of the coordination shell of both ions are labelled and coloured.
                    - generic [ref=e1295]:
                      - text: The catalytic core of [FeFe]-hydrogenases consists of four domains
                      - link "[133]" [ref=e1296] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0665"
                        - generic [ref=e1297]: "[133]"
                      - text: ", which help with electron transfer to and from the active site and cofactors. The active site of [FeFe]-hydrogenases consists of a 4Fe-4S cluster ligated by four Cys, which is coordinated"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1298]: via
                      - text: a single conserved Cys thiolate sulfur atom to the two Fe core. Each of those two iron ions is attached to a CO and CN
                      - superscript [ref=e1299]: −
                      - text: ligand and a bridging CO ligand
                      - link "[134]" [ref=e1300] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0670"
                        - generic [ref=e1301]: "[134]"
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1302]:
                      - text: A small number of NAD(P)H-dependent hydrogenases have been described. All of the NAD(P)H-dependent hydrogenases are multimeric enzyme complexes
                      - link "[135]" [ref=e1303] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0675"
                        - generic [ref=e1304]: "[135]"
                      - text: ", including a subunit with a flavin moiety which carries out the oxidation/reduction reaction of the nicotinamide cofactor. The best studied member is the bidirectional soluble [NiFe]-hydrogenase of"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1305]: Cupriavidus necator
                      - text: H16
                      - link "[136]" [ref=e1306] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0680"
                        - generic [ref=e1307]: "[136]"
                      - text: ", which oxidizes or produces H"
                      - subscript [ref=e1308]: "2"
                      - text: in response to changes in cytoplasmic reduction states.
                  - generic [ref=e1309]:
                    - 'heading "4.12. EC 1.13 and 1.14: oxygenases and monooxygenases" [level=3] [ref=e1310]'
                    - generic [ref=e1311]: Monooxygenases catalyze the transfer of a single atom of oxygen to a substrate, requiring first that the oxygen molecule be activated, which takes place by transferring electrons to the oxygen molecule. Depending on the type of monooxygenase, electrons can be donated either from the substrate or from a cofactor, which determines the type of reactive oxygen intermediate. Here, we focus on NAD(P)H-dependent monooxygenases.
                    - generic [ref=e1312]: Cytochrome P450 are haem B monooxygenases (CYPs), which are able to catalyze a wide range of reactions (such as epoxidations, hydroxylations, heteroatom-dealkylations/oxidations, dehalogenations, dehydrations, reductions, dehalogenations and oxidative deaminations). The haem group activates molecular oxygen by transferring electrons to it, which in the case of NAD(P)H-dependent CYPs are ultimately provided by NAD(P)H. The mechanism by which the electrons are transferred to the haem group from the nicotinamide cofactor varies between different CYPs.
                    - generic [ref=e1313]:
                      - text: The general mechanism of NAD(P)H-dependent CYPs can be divided into four main steps. Firstly, the substrate binds to the enzyme, and an electron is transferred to the haem group from NAD(P)H
                      - emphasis [ref=e1314]: via
                      - text: a flavin-containing cytochrome P450 reductase, to reduce Fe
                      - superscript [ref=e1315]: 3 +
                      - text: to Fe
                      - superscript [ref=e1316]: 2 +
                      - text: . Secondly, an oxygen molecule binds to the complex, forming a complex known as oxy-P450. Thirdly, the transfer of a second electron and two protons to the oxy-P450 complex causes the cleavage of the O
                      - img "single bond" [ref=e1317]
                      - text: O bond, leading to the formation of a reactive intermediate and a water molecule. Then, the reactive intermediate inserts the oxygen atom into the substrate. Finally, product dissociation regenerates the initial state of the enzyme. In this state, a molecule of water coordinates the Fe atom of the haem-group as an axial ligand. The other ligands of the Fe atom are the nitrogen atoms of the haem group and a Cys residue present in the active site of all CYPs, which makes it more reactive
                      - link "[137]" [ref=e1318] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0685"
                        - generic [ref=e1319]: "[137]"
                      - text: . The effect of the Cys ligand on reactivity is due to two factors, it lowers the reduction potential of the active site, and it increases the pKa of the oxygen-bound intermediate, which facilitates its protonation, resulting in the cleavage of the O
                      - img "single bond" [ref=e1320]
                      - text: O bond
                      - link "[138]" [ref=e1321] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0690"
                        - generic [ref=e1322]: "[138]"
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1323]:
                      - text: In most CYP systems, the cytochrome P450 component and the reductase component, which transfers the electrons to the cytochrome P450 from the electron donor, are separate proteins. However, some simpler systems have been found in which both components are fused in a single polypeptide chain, making heterologous expression and protein engineering more facile. An example of this is the well-characterized BM3 enzyme from
                      - emphasis [ref=e1324]: Bacillus megaterium
                      - text: ", which is a soluble enzyme able to hydroxylate fatty acids of varying length between 12 and 18 carbon atoms, as well as the corresponding amides and alcohols. BM3 also displays a weak hydroxylation activity towards shorter chain alkanes, and has been modified, by means of random mutagenesis, to obtain several variants with enhanced activity towards octane, being up to five times more efficient than the wild type enzyme. One of the variants contained a single point mutation of a Glu residue in the surface of the protein by a His residue. The effect of this mutation was surprising, given the fact that this residue is located relatively far from the haem group"
                      - link "[139]" [ref=e1325] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0695"
                        - generic [ref=e1326]: "[139]"
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1327]:
                      - text: Flavin-dependent monooxygenases catalyze a very wide range of oxidative reactions, and use either FMN or FAD as cofactors. In order to introduce an oxygen atom to the substrate, oxygen first binds to the flavin in its reduced form, to form peroxyflavin or hydroperoxyflavin. Then, the flavin derivative performs a nucleophilic or an electrophilic attack, depending on the protonation state of the flavin derivative, which leads to the oxygenation of the substrate, the production of one water molecule and the formation of the oxidized state of the flavin. The role of the NAD(P)H is to restore the reduced form of the flavin in the catalytic site
                      - link "[37]" [ref=e1328] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0185"
                        - generic [ref=e1329]: "[37]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[140]" [ref=e1330] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0700"
                        - generic [ref=e1331]: "[140]"
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1332]:
                      - text: There are also some NAD(P)H-dependent monooxygenases where the cofactor that activates O
                      - subscript [ref=e1333]: "2"
                      - text: is not a haem group or a flavin, but other redox cofactors. An example is methane monooxygenase (MMO), which catalyzes the incorporation of an oxygen atom into methane (it is also active with other hydrocarbons), yielding methanol, which is the critical first step in methanotrophic carbon assimilation. In MMO, activation of molecular oxygen is carried out by a metallic cofactor consisting of two Fe ions each coordinated by six ligands (a combination of His and Glu residues and water molecules)
                      - link "[141]" [ref=e1334] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0705"
                        - generic [ref=e1335]: "[141]"
                      - text: . After one oxygen atom is transferred to the substrate, the reduced form of the metallic centre is regenerated by the transfer of two electrons from NAD(P)H
                      - link "[142]" [ref=e1336] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0710"
                        - generic [ref=e1337]: "[142]"
                      - text: ". There are two types of MMO, soluble MMO (sMMO) and membrane-bound particulate MMO (pMMO). Soluble MMO is dependent on NAD(P)H, and contains three subunits: MmoA, MmoB and MmoC. MmoA carries out the oxygenation reaction, while MmoC binds NAD(P)H and provides to MmoA the electrons required for the reaction through MmoB"
                      - link "[143]" [ref=e1338] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0715"
                        - generic [ref=e1339]: "[143]"
                      - text: . MMO has been extensively studied thanks to its ability to oxidize a variety of hydrocarbons under mild conditions, and carry out a gas-to-liquid transformation for natural gas (methane to methanol). However, protein engineering studies have been hindered by the fact that no functional MMO has been expressed in
                      - emphasis [ref=e1340]: E
                      - text: .
                      - emphasis [ref=e1341]: coli
                      - link "[144]" [ref=e1342] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0720"
                        - generic [ref=e1343]: "[144]"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e1344]:
                    - 'heading "4.13. EC 1.16.1: oxidoreductases of metal ions" [level=3] [ref=e1345]'
                    - generic [ref=e1346]: This class of enzymes comprises NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases able to transfer electrons to or from metallic ions. The metallic substrate can be in a free form (as in the case of mercury reductase), chelated by relatively small molecules (for example, ferric-chelate reductase or aquacobalamin reductase) or forming part of another protein (such as methionine synthase reductase and transferrin reductase).
                    - generic [ref=e1347]:
                      - text: In general, when NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases of metal ions act to reduce the metal, they first transfer the electrons from NAD(P)H to FAD, and then the reduced FAD donates the electrons to the metallic ion. This electron transfer path allows the enzyme to transfer the electrons provided by NAD(P)H to the metallic atoms one at a time, as in most cases they require the transfer of one single electron in order to be reduced or oxidized. Mercuric reductase and cyanocobalamin reductase are exceptions, as they perform two-electron reduction of Hg
                      - superscript [ref=e1348]: 2 +
                      - text: to Hg
                      - superscript [ref=e1349]: "0"
                      - text: and Co
                      - superscript [ref=e1350]: 3 +
                      - text: to Co
                      - superscript [ref=e1351]: +
                      - text: ", respectively, with FAD as a cofactor. Human methionine synthase reductase, which reduces the Co"
                      - superscript [ref=e1352]: 2 +
                      - text: atom of the cobalamin cofactor found in methionine synthase, follows a variation of this path where electrons are first transferred from NADPH to FAD, then to FMN and finally to the metal
                      - link "[145]" [ref=e1353] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0725"
                        - generic [ref=e1354]: "[145]"
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1355]:
                      - generic [ref=e1356]:
                        - text: Mercuric reductase is an enzyme of particular interest for phytoremediation, since it could potentially be used to generate plants capable of detoxifying mercuric compounds. Many soil and enteric bacteria have a detoxification system for organomercuric compounds where mercuric reductase plays a key role. This system allows these organisms to generate volatile mercury (Hg
                        - superscript [ref=e1357]: "0"
                        - text: ) from ionic mercury (Hg
                        - superscript [ref=e1358]: 2 +
                        - text: ) by a two-electron reduction, thereby avoiding toxicity associated with the tendency of Hg
                        - superscript [ref=e1359]: 2 +
                        - text: to cause sulfhydryl chelation
                        - link "[146]" [ref=e1360] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0730"
                          - generic [ref=e1361]: "[146]"
                        - text: . The first step of the reaction is the binding of Hg
                        - superscript [ref=e1362]: 2 +
                        - text: to the active site of the enzyme, where it is chelated by the thiolate groups of two deprotonated Cys residues. Then, two electrons are transferred from NADPH to FAD. Finally, the reduced FAD reduces the Cys-Hg
                        - superscript [ref=e1363]: 2 +
                        - text: "-Cys complex, and the resulting Hg"
                        - superscript [ref=e1364]: "0"
                        - text: is released
                        - link "[147]" [ref=e1365] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0735"
                          - generic [ref=e1366]: "[147]"
                        - text: (
                        - link "Fig. 12" [ref=e1367] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#f0060"
                          - generic [ref=e1368]: Fig. 12
                        - text: ).
                      - figure [ref=e1369]:
                        - generic [ref=e1370]:
                          - img "Fig. 12"
                          - list [ref=e1371]:
                            - listitem [ref=e1372]:
                              - 'link "Download: Download high-res image (355KB)" [ref=e1373] [cursor=pointer]':
                                - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S157096391730273X-gr12_lrg.jpg
                                - generic [ref=e1375]: "Download: Download high-res image (355KB)"
                            - listitem [ref=e1376]:
                              - 'link "Download: Download full-size image" [ref=e1377] [cursor=pointer]':
                                - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S157096391730273X-gr12.jpg
                                - generic [ref=e1379]: "Download: Download full-size image"
                        - paragraph [ref=e1382]:
                          - text: Fig. 12. Active site of
                          - emphasis [ref=e1383]: Pseudomonas aeruginosa
                          - text: NADPH-dependent mercuric reductase (PDB code
                          - link "4K7ZOpens in new window" [ref=e1384] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: http://www.rcsb.org/pdb/explore.do?structureId=4K7Z
                            - generic [ref=e1385]:
                              - text: 4K7Z
                              - img "Opens in new window" [ref=e1386]
                          - text: ). Substrate is displayed in purple, NADP
                          - superscript [ref=e1388]: +
                          - text: and FAD are displayed in grey and light blue respectively with heteroatoms coloured independently and the cysteines that coordinate Hg
                          - superscript [ref=e1389]: 2 +
                          - text: ", essential for enzyme activity, are labelled and coloured in dark grey."
                  - generic [ref=e1390]:
                    - 'heading "4.14. EC 1.17.1: oxidoreductases acting on CH or CH2 groups" [level=3] [ref=e1391]':
                      - text: "4.14. EC 1.17.1: oxidoreductases acting on CH or CH"
                      - subscript [ref=e1392]: "2"
                      - text: groups
                    - generic [ref=e1393]:
                      - text: These oxidoreductases catalyze the oxidation or reduction of CH or CH
                      - subscript [ref=e1394]: "2"
                      - text: groups, which in most cases results in the addition or removal of a hydroxy group. The only exception is xanthine dehydrogenase, which catalyzes the addition of an oxo group.
                    - generic [ref=e1395]:
                      - generic [ref=e1396]:
                        - text: Xanthine dehydrogenase (EC 1.7.1.4) and nicotinate dehydrogenase (EC 1.7.1.5), which catalyze respectively the oxidation of xanthine to urate with NAD
                        - superscript [ref=e1397]: +
                        - text: as the electron acceptor and the oxidation of nicotinate to 6-hydroxynicotinate with NADP
                        - superscript [ref=e1398]: +
                        - text: ", employ similar reaction mechanisms. Both contain a molybdenum cofactor (molybdopterin), two Fe-S clusters of the [2Fe-2S] type and FAD"
                        - link "[148]" [ref=e1399] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0740"
                          - generic [ref=e1400]: "[148]"
                        - text: . All of these redox centres are positioned linearly, and close enough to allow sequential transfer of electrons through them. After substrate binding, an OH ligand of the Mo is deprotonated by a Glu residue. The resulting Mo
                        - img "single bond" [ref=e1401]
                        - text: O
                        - superscript [ref=e1402]:
                          - img "single bond" [ref=e1403]
                        - text: performs a nucleophilic attack on the carbon atom to be hydroxylated and a hydride group is transferred to the Mo cofactor, reducing Mo
                        - superscript [ref=e1404]: 6 +
                        - text: to Mo
                        - superscript [ref=e1405]: 4 +
                        - text: . Then, the bond between the Mo cofactor and the substrate is hydrolysed, releasing the free hydroxylated product. Finally, the hydride groups are transferred first to the Fe-S centers, then to FAD and eventually to NAD(P)
                        - superscript [ref=e1406]: +
                        - text: to yield NAD(P)H
                        - link "[148]" [ref=e1407] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0740"
                          - generic [ref=e1408]: "[148]"
                        - text: (
                        - link "Fig. 13" [ref=e1409] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#f0065"
                          - generic [ref=e1410]: Fig. 13
                        - text: ).
                      - figure [ref=e1411]:
                        - generic [ref=e1412]:
                          - img "Fig. 13"
                          - list [ref=e1413]:
                            - listitem [ref=e1414]:
                              - 'link "Download: Download high-res image (467KB)" [ref=e1415] [cursor=pointer]':

                              - 'link "Download: Download full-size image" [ref=e1419] [cursor=pointer]':

                        - paragraph [ref=e1424]:
                          - text: Fig. 13. Overview of cofactors involved in electron transfer in bovine milk xanthine dehydrogenase (PDB code
                          - link "3BDJOpens in new window" [ref=e1425] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: http://www.rcsb.org/pdb/explore.do?structureId=3BDJ
                            - generic [ref=e1426]:
                              - text: 3BDJ
                              - img "Opens in new window" [ref=e1427]
                          - text: and
                          - link "3UNAOpens in new window" [ref=e1429] [cursor=pointer]:

                            - generic [ref=e1430]:

                          - text: ). Oxopurinol, an inhibitor that binds to the same site than the substrate, is displayed in purple; molybdopterine, FAD and NAD
                          - superscript [ref=e1433]: +
                          - text: are displayed in dark green, blue and grey respectively; heteroatoms are coloured individually.
                    - generic [ref=e1434]:
                      - text: Leucoanthocyanidin reductase (EC 1.17.1.3), which catalyzes the reduction of 2
                      - emphasis [ref=e1435]: R
                      - text: ",3"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1436]: S
                      - text: ",4"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1437]: S
                      - text: "-flavan-3,4-diols to 2"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1438]: R
                      - text: ",3"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1439]: S
                      - text: "-flavan-3-ols (for example, leucocyanidin to catechin) coupled to the oxidation of NADPH to NADP"
                      - superscript [ref=e1440]: +
                      - text: ", uses a different mechanism. Firstly, a step of dehydration of the substrate takes place. In order to do so, a Lys residue deprotonates the phenolic O7 atom of the substrate. This makes the hydroxyl group of C4 act as a leaving group which takes a proton from the adjacent O5. The O5 atom recovers the proton from a His residue. This generates a quinone methide intermediate, to which a hydride is transferred directly from NADPH to generate catechin and NADP"
                      - superscript [ref=e1441]: +
                      - link "[44]" [ref=e1442] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0220"
                        - generic [ref=e1443]: "[44]"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e1444]:
                    - 'heading "4.15. EC 1.18.1 and 1.19.1: oxidoreductases of iron-sulfur proteins and of flavodoxin" [level=3] [ref=e1445]'
                    - generic [ref=e1446]:
                      - generic [ref=e1447]:
                        - text: This group of oxidoreductases are able to catalyze the reduction and oxidation of the Fe-S clusters of iron-sulfur proteins using NAD(P)H/NAD(P)
                        - superscript [ref=e1448]: +
                        - text: as the electron donor/acceptor. The iron-sulfur proteins on which they can act include ferredoxin, adrenodoxin, putidaredoxin and rubredoxin, with the latter usually being classified as an iron-sulfur protein even though it does not contain a canonical Fe-S cluster but instead contains a lone iron ion coordinated by several Cys residues. Iron-sulfur proteins can have very different redox potentials, and can thus participate in a variety of redox reactions. Consequently, the physiological role they play is quite varied, including participating as electron carriers in photosynthesis
                        - link "[149]" [ref=e1449] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0745"
                          - generic [ref=e1450]: "[149]"
                        - text: ", donating electrons to cytochrome P450 for steroid biosynthesis"
                        - link "[150]" [ref=e1451] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0750"
                          - generic [ref=e1452]: "[150]"
                        - text: or providing electrons to carry out hydroxylation reactions during ω-oxidation of fatty acids
                        - link "[151]" [ref=e1453] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0755"
                          - generic [ref=e1454]: "[151]"
                        - text: . All NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases of iron-sulfur proteins require an FAD cofactor, which acts as an intermediary in the transfer of electrons. This is due to the fact that iron-sulfur proteins are one-electron donors/acceptors, while NAD(P)H/NAD(P)
                        - superscript [ref=e1455]: +
                        - text: only participate in two-electron transfers. Since FAD can participate both in one-electron transfers and two-electron transfers, it can first receive two electrons from NAD(P)H to be reduced to its hydroquinone fully reduced form, and then transfer them one at a time to the substrate, being oxidized first to a semiquinone form and finally to the fully oxidized quinone form. Structures of these enzymes show FAD and NAD(P)H/NAD(P)
                        - superscript [ref=e1456]: +
                        - text: are close to each other, suggesting direct electron transfer between both redox cofactors (
                        - link "Fig. 14" [ref=e1457] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#f0070"
                          - generic [ref=e1458]: Fig. 14
                        - text: ). The enzymes differ on the route followed by electrons from the Fe-S cluster to FAD, with some of them involving several intermediate residue side chains
                        - link "[152]" [ref=e1459] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0760"
                          - generic [ref=e1460]: "[152]"
                        - text: . Additionally, the NADP
                        - superscript [ref=e1461]: +
                        - text: "-dependent ferredoxin oxidoreductase of some organisms are also able to catalyze redox reactions with flavodoxin (EC 1.19.1.1), another small, FMN-containing protein which participates in several redox reactions and can replace ferredoxin in some of its functions under low iron availability conditions"
                        - link "[153]" [ref=e1462] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0765"
                          - generic [ref=e1463]: "[153]"
                        - text: . This is the case, for example, of
                        - emphasis [ref=e1464]: E
                        - text: .
                        - emphasis [ref=e1465]: coli
                        - text: and
                        - emphasis [ref=e1466]: Anabaena
                        - text: ferredoxin-NADP
                        - superscript [ref=e1467]: +
                        - text: reductase
                        - link "[49]" [ref=e1468] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0245"
                          - generic [ref=e1469]: "[49]"
                        - text: .
                      - figure [ref=e1470]:

                        - paragraph [ref=e1483]:
                          - text: Fig. 14. Interface of the complex between maize ferredoxin and ferredoxin reductase (PDB code
                          - link "1GAQOpens in new window" [ref=e1484] [cursor=pointer]:

                            - generic [ref=e1485]:

                          - link "1GJROpens in new window" [ref=e1488] [cursor=pointer]:

                            - generic [ref=e1489]:
