                  - generic [ref=e1492]:
                    - 'heading "4.16. EC 1.20.1: oxidoreductases of phosphorus or arsenic compounds" [level=3] [ref=e1493]'
                    - generic [ref=e1494]:
                      - text: Phosphonate dehydrogenase (EC 1.20.1.1), also called phosphite dehydrogenase (PTDH), catalyzes the oxidation of phosphonates or phosphite to phosphates. During the reaction, electrons are transferred to NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1495]: +
                      - text: ", generating NADH."
                    - generic [ref=e1496]:
                      - generic [ref=e1497]:
                        - text: "The exact mechanism through which PTDH catalyzes the reduction of phosphite remains unclear, but there are some proposed reaction mechanisms based on the high degree of sequence homology between PTDH and 2-D-hydroxyacid dehydrogenases. There are three residues essential for catalysis: a His, a Glu and an Arg ("
                        - link "Fig. 15" [ref=e1498] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#f0075"
                          - generic [ref=e1499]: Fig. 15
                        - text: ). The His residue has been proposed to act as a general base to abstract a proton from a water molecule, generating a hydroxide ion. The hydroxide ion then carries out a nucleophilic attack on phosphite, and at the same time a hydride leaving group is transferred to NAD
                        - superscript [ref=e1500]: +
                        - text: . The Glu residue probably helps to orient the His residue for catalysis and modulates its pKa to facilitate its role as a general base. Finally, the Arg residue is believed to be essential for binding the substrate and correctly orienting it. Mutations in any of these residues have been found to compromise catalytic efficiency
                        - link "[154]" [ref=e1501] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0770"
                          - generic [ref=e1502]: "[154]"
                        - text: ","
                        - link "[155]" [ref=e1503] [cursor=pointer]:
                          - /url: "#bb0775"
                          - generic [ref=e1504]: "[155]"
                        - text: .
                      - figure [ref=e1505]:
                        - generic [ref=e1506]:
                          - img "Fig. 15"
                          - list [ref=e1507]:
                            - listitem [ref=e1508]:
                              - 'link "Download: Download high-res image (234KB)" [ref=e1509] [cursor=pointer]':
                                - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S157096391730273X-gr15_lrg.jpg
                                - generic [ref=e1511]: "Download: Download high-res image (234KB)"
                            - listitem [ref=e1512]:
                              - 'link "Download: Download full-size image" [ref=e1513] [cursor=pointer]':
                                - /url: https://ars.els-cdn.com/content/image/1-s2.0-S157096391730273X-gr15.jpg
                                - generic [ref=e1515]: "Download: Download full-size image"
                        - paragraph [ref=e1518]:
                          - text: Fig. 15. Active site of
                          - emphasis [ref=e1519]: Pseudomonas stutzeri
                          - text: phosphite dehydrogenase (PDB code
                          - link "4E5KOpens in new window" [ref=e1520] [cursor=pointer]:
                            - /url: http://www.rcsb.org/pdb/explore.do?structureId=4E5K
                            - generic [ref=e1521]:
                              - text: 4E5K
                              - img "Opens in new window" [ref=e1522]
                          - text: ). Sulfite, an inhibitor of the enzyme which binds in the same way than the substrate does, is displayed in purple; NAD
                          - superscript [ref=e1524]: +
                          - text: is displayed in grey with heteroatoms coloured independently and residues that are essential for enzyme activity are labelled and coloured in dark grey.
                  - generic [ref=e1525]:
                    - 'heading "4.17. EC 1.21.1: reductive dehalogenases" [level=3] [ref=e1526]'
                    - generic [ref=e1527]: "There are two enzymes classified as NAD(P)H-dependent reductive dehalogenases: iodotyrosine deiodinase and 2,4-dichlorobenzoyl-CoA reductase."
                    - generic [ref=e1528]:
                      - text: Iodotyrosine deiodinase catalyzes the reductive dehalogenation of mono- and diiodotyrosine side-products generated during the biosynthesis of thyroxine (the prohormone of triiodothyronine, the most active thyroid hormone). The reaction involves the removal of the iodine substituents from the tyrosine derivative and their reduction to iodide ions, coupled to the oxidation of NADPH to NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e1529]: +
                      - text: . This process allows the scavenging of iodide, which can then be used for the synthesis of more thyroid hormones or other processes. Defects in this enzyme can lead to iodide deficiency, which can have several detrimental effects to health such as hypothyroidism. The mechanism employed by iodotyrosine deiodinase has not still been fully established, although recent structures of the enzyme bound to its substrate have provided some insight
                      - link "[51]" [ref=e1530] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0255"
                        - generic [ref=e1531]: "[51]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[156]" [ref=e1532] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0780"
                        - generic [ref=e1533]: "[156]"
                      - text: . The enzyme contains an FMN cofactor which plays a key role in the catalytic process by carrying out a stepwise reduction of the substrate by means of sequential one-electron transfers and not through a single two-electron transfer as previously proposed
                      - link "[51]" [ref=e1534] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0255"
                        - generic [ref=e1535]: "[51]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[157]" [ref=e1536] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0785"
                        - generic [ref=e1537]: "[157]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[158]" [ref=e1538] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0790"
                        - generic [ref=e1539]: "[158]"
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1540]:
                      - text: 2,4-Dichlorobenzoyl-CoA reductase was identified in
                      - emphasis [ref=e1541]: Corynebacterium sepedonicum
                      - text: ", an organism able to grow with 2,4-dichlorobenzoate as the sole source of energy and carbon, which could be of potential interest for the remediation of sites contaminated by polychlorinated biphenyl"
                      - link "[159]" [ref=e1542] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0795"
                        - generic [ref=e1543]: "[159]"
                      - text: . In order to catabolise 2,4-dichlorobenzoate, it is first converted to the corresponding CoA thioester, 2,4-dichlorobenzoyl-CoA. The thioester is then subject to a reductive dehalogenation catalyzed by 2,4-dichlorobenzoyl-CoA reductase, where NADPH provides the electrons. This reaction leads to the production of 4-chlorobenzoyl-CoA, which undergoes an additional dehalogenation (independent of NAD(P)H) before the thioester bond is hydrolyzed. Subsequent hydroxylations and oxygenations complete the catabolic pathway. The reaction mechanism of 2,4-dichlorobenzoyl-CoA reductase has been speculated to involve a nucleophilic attack of the C2 position of the substrate by a hydride group, with the Cl atom acting as a leaving group. However, this has not been confirmed. It is also unclear whether the hydride group would be transferred directly from NADPH or from an intermediate species
                      - link "[159]" [ref=e1544] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0795"
                        - generic [ref=e1545]: "[159]"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e1546]:
                    - 'heading "4.18. EC 1.23.1: oxidoreductases reducing Csingle bondOsingle bondC group" [level=3] [ref=e1547]':
                      - text: "4.18. EC 1.23.1: oxidoreductases reducing C"
                      - img "single bond" [ref=e1548]
                      - text: O
                      - img "single bond" [ref=e1549]
                      - text: C group
                    - generic [ref=e1550]:
                      - text: Pinoresinol-lariciresinol reductase (EC 1.23.1.1–4) is a multifunctional enzyme found in certain plants (such as
                      - emphasis [ref=e1551]: Forsythia
                      - text: and
                      - emphasis [ref=e1552]: Thuja
                      - text: ) able to catalyze the reduction of pinoresinol to lariciresinol and of lariciresinol to secoisolariciresinol, with NADPH providing the required electrons. This enzyme is unique in its ability to directly reduce an ether group to an alcohol. The crystal structure of the enzyme from
                      - emphasis [ref=e1553]: Thuja plicata
                      - text: has been determined, and shows that the NADPH and substrate binding pockets are close enough for direct electron transfer to occur. The exact mechanism and reaction intermediates through which catalysis proceeds are still unknown due to the lack of published structures of enzymes with substrate bound
                      - link "[53]" [ref=e1554] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0265"
                        - generic [ref=e1555]: "[53]"
                      - text: .
                - generic [ref=e1556]:
                  - heading "5. Metabolic and protein engineering of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases" [level=2] [ref=e1557]
                  - generic [ref=e1558]:
                    - heading "5.1. Metabolic engineering" [level=3] [ref=e1559]
                    - generic [ref=e1560]:
                      - text: There have been numerous metabolic engineering studies in which the metabolic network of an organism has been altered by the addition of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductase(s), or pathways including them
                      - link "[100]" [ref=e1561] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0500"
                        - generic [ref=e1562]: "[100]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[160]" [ref=e1563] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0800"
                        - generic [ref=e1564]: "[160]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[161]" [ref=e1565] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0805"
                        - generic [ref=e1566]: "[161]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[162]" [ref=e1567] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0810"
                        - generic [ref=e1568]: "[162]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[163]" [ref=e1569] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0815"
                        - generic [ref=e1570]: "[163]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[164]" [ref=e1571] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0820"
                        - generic [ref=e1572]: "[164]"
                      - text: . One of the most frequent goals of this type of approach is the efficient production of a given chemical of interest without the need for chemical synthesis or purifying enzymes, and ideally from renewable feedstocks and with a lower energy consumption due to the mild conditions at which reactions can be catalyzed by NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases. However, metabolic engineering provides a useful tool not only for this purpose, but also others such as improved biodegradation
                      - link "[165]" [ref=e1573] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0825"
                        - generic [ref=e1574]: "[165]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[166]" [ref=e1575] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0830"
                        - generic [ref=e1576]: "[166]"
                      - text: . This is usually achieved by genetically manipulating a candidate organism to add, modify (commonly by over-expression) and/or remove the genes coding for one or more enzymes.
                    - generic [ref=e1577]:
                      - text: An example of successful metabolic engineering leading to the development of a synthesis process suitable for commercial application was the construction of an
                      - emphasis [ref=e1578]: E
                      - text: .
                      - emphasis [ref=e1579]: coli
                      - text: strain able to produce 1,3-propanediol (a precursor for the generation of several polymers, including fibers) at high titer when growing aerobically and using only glucose as the substrate
                      - link "[167]" [ref=e1580] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0835"
                        - generic [ref=e1581]: "[167]"
                      - text: . Several additions and deletions of genes of
                      - emphasis [ref=e1582]: E
                      - text: .
                      - emphasis [ref=e1583]: coli
                      - text: K12 were carried out to divert carbon from dihydroxyacetone phosphate (DHAP), generated during glycolysis, to 1,3-propanediol. Genes encoding glycerol 3-phosphate dehydrogenase and glycerol 3-phosphate phosphatase from
                      - emphasis [ref=e1584]: Saccharomyces cerevisiae
                      - text: were introduced in the original strain, which allow the generation of glycerol from DHAP. Additionally, genes encoding glycerol dehydratase from
                      - emphasis [ref=e1585]: Klebsiella pneumoniae
                      - text: were also introduced, which catalyze the conversion of glycerol to 3-hydroxypropionaldehyde. The final step of reduction to 1,3-propanediol was found to be carried out by the endogenous oxidoreductase
                      - emphasis [ref=e1586]: yqhD
                      - text: . In order to improve the 1,3-propanediol yield, genes encoding glycerol kinase and glycerol dehydrogenase were deleted. This prevented glycerol from being redirected to the main glycolytic pathway.
                    - generic [ref=e1587]:
                      - text: In a more recent study, an
                      - emphasis [ref=e1588]: E
                      - text: .
                      - emphasis [ref=e1589]: coli
                      - text: strain able to produce 1,4-butanediol (BDO) was generated. This study combined addition, removal and modification of enzymes, showing how different approaches can be combined to achieve optimal results
                      - link "[168]" [ref=e1590] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0840"
                        - generic [ref=e1591]: "[168]"
                      - text: ". Firstly, a set of candidate pathways for the synthesis of BDO from several central metabolites was computationally identified and ranked according to several criteria, such as thermodynamic parameters, pathway length or number of non-native steps. A pathway to generate BDO from succinate was chosen and implemented. The pathway involved six reactions, catalyzed by succinyl-CoA synthetase, CoA-dependent succinate semialdehyde dehydrogenase, 4-hydroxybutyrate dehydrogenase, 4-hydroxybutyryl-CoA transferase, 4-hydroxybutyryl-CoA reductase and an alcohol dehydrogenase able to reduce 4-hydroxybutyraldehyde to BDO. All of the enzymes except succinyl-CoA synthetase and 4-hydroxybutyryl-CoA transferase were NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases. An additional gene containing α-ketoglutarate decarboxylase was also added, which allowed the conversion of α-ketoglutarate into one of the intermediates of the pathway. In order to increase the BDO yield, the genes coding certain enzymes were deleted to divert carbon and energy towards its production:"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1592]: E
                      - text: .
                      - emphasis [ref=e1593]: coli
                      - text: alcohol dehydrogenase, pyruvate formate lyase, lactate dehydrogenase and malate dehydrogenase. Finally, protein engineering was used to introduce a R163L mutation in citrate synthetase, which reduced the inhibition of this enzyme by NADH. This improved the TCA-cycle flux, enhancing the production of α-ketoglutarate. The resulting strain was able to produce relatively high titers of BDO when grown microaerobically in M9 medium supplemented with glucose, xylose, sucrose or mixed sugars.
                    - generic [ref=e1594]:
                      - text: Another interesting possibility is engineering cells to modify the balance of reduced and oxidized cofactors, such as NADH/NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1595]: +
                      - text: . This could further optimize the production of metabolites when the limiting factor becomes the availability of a specific form of the cofactor. Berríos-Rivera et al. managed to achieve an increased NADH/NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1596]: +
                      - text: ratio in
                      - emphasis [ref=e1597]: E
                      - text: .
                      - emphasis [ref=e1598]: coli
                      - text: by substituting the native, cofactor-independent formate dehydrogenase with a NADH-dependent formate dehydrogenase. Interestingly, the increased ratio of NADH/NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1599]: +
                      - text: not only caused an increased production of reduced fermentative products under anaerobic conditions (the ethanol-acetate ratio was greatly increased), but also an activation of fermentative pathways even under aerobic conditions
                      - link "[169]" [ref=e1600] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0845"
                        - generic [ref=e1601]: "[169]"
                      - text: .
                  - generic [ref=e1602]:
                    - heading "5.2. Protein engineering" [level=3] [ref=e1603]
                    - generic [ref=e1604]: Various properties of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases have been targeted by protein engineering and/or directed evolution approaches to make them more suitable for applications. These include enhancing the kinetic properties of enzymes, increasing their stability and modifying the specificity of enzymes for their substrate or cofactor.
                    - generic [ref=e1605]:
                      - text: The improvement of kinetic properties is of interest not only for improving the natural reaction of a given oxidoreductase, but also when an enzyme with a broad substrate specificity is available but the activity towards a specific substrate of interest is low. There are a few cases of oxidoreductases whose kinetic properties for either the main substrate or secondary substrates have been improved. For example, Moon & Liu engineered an NADH-dependent methylglyoxal reductase from
                      - emphasis [ref=e1606]: Saccharomyces cerevisiae
                      - text: (GRE2) to have enhanced aldehyde reductase activity towards furfural and 5-hydroxymethylfurfural
                      - link "[170]" [ref=e1607] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0850"
                        - generic [ref=e1608]: "[170]"
                      - text: . From a library of variants generated by means of error-prone PCR, two variants with the enhanced desired activity were identified. Interestingly, one of these variants also acquired the ability to use NADPH as a cofactor in addition to NADH. In another study, Li et al. aimed to improve the 3-hydroxypropionaldehyde (3-HPA) reductase activity of a 1,3-propanediol oxidoreductase (YqhD) with the goal of obtaining an efficient enzyme for the biological production of 1,3-propanediol
                      - link "[167]" [ref=e1609] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0835"
                        - generic [ref=e1610]: "[167]"
                      - text: . After screening a library of mutants generated through error-prone PCR, two variants with improved kinetic properties were isolated. They displayed both a lower K
                      - subscript [ref=e1611]: m
                      - text: and a higher k
                      - subscript [ref=e1612]: cat
                      - text: towards 3-HPA.
                    - generic [ref=e1613]:
                      - text: There have also been multiple attempts to modify the specificity of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases for the cofactor they use for the electron transfer reaction, as controlling the cofactor specificity of this class of enzymes can be used to optimally engineer the cellular metabolism by achieving a better balance of cofactor availability
                      - link "[171]" [ref=e1614] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0855"
                        - generic [ref=e1615]: "[171]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[172]" [ref=e1616] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0860"
                        - generic [ref=e1617]: "[172]"
                      - text: . During the last two decades, there have been a considerable number of successful cases of relaxation or even reversal of NADH/NADPH cofactor specificity. Altering an NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductase to use NADH/NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1618]: +
                      - text: instead is of great interest for non-cell-based industrial applications since the phosphorylated cofactors are significantly more expensive and less stable than their nonphosphorylated counterparts, making the enzymes that are dependent on NADPH/NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e1619]: +
                      - text: less attractive for industrial-scale applications
                      - link "[173]" [ref=e1620] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0865"
                        - generic [ref=e1621]: "[173]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[174]" [ref=e1622] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0870"
                        - generic [ref=e1623]: "[174]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[175]" [ref=e1624] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0875"
                        - generic [ref=e1625]: "[175]"
                      - text: . Examples of this include an NADPH-specific xylose reductase from
                      - emphasis [ref=e1626]: Candida boidinii
                      - text: which was engineered to be able to use also NADH by means of computational design through minimization of the binding energy of the cofactor in variants were the residues of the cofactor binding pocket were mutated. A set of 10 candidate variants were identified, which were experimentally tested, leading to seven variants able to reduce xylose with NADH
                      - link "[176]" [ref=e1627] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0880"
                        - generic [ref=e1628]: "[176]"
                      - text: . In general, cofactor specificity reversal must be done on a case-by-case basis. However, there have been some attempts at developing general methodologies for specific subsets of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases. For example, Brinkmann-Chen et al. managed to develop a general approach to reverse the cofactor specificity of ketol-acid reductoisomerases from NADPH to NADH combining information derived from crystal structures and multiple sequence alignments. By applying this approach, they obtained variants for several enzymes of this class whose catalytic efficiency with NADH was comparable to that of the wild type enzymes with NADPH or even higher
                      - link "[177]" [ref=e1629] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0885"
                        - generic [ref=e1630]: "[177]"
                      - text: . This is noteworthy, as other studies attempting to change the cofactor specificity of an NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductase typically report engineered variants which do not reach a catalytic efficiency with the new cofactor as high as that of the original enzyme with its native cofactor. The opposite cofactor specificity reversal from NADH/NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1631]: +
                      - text: to NADPH/NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e1632]: +
                      - text: has also been reported. This type of reversal has been applied to generate enzymes suitable to be used in systems where regeneration of NADPH is necessary. With this purpose, Lerchner et al. engineered the highly NADH-specific alanine dehydrogenase from
                      - emphasis [ref=e1633]: Bacillus subtilis
                      - text: to use NADPH with nearly the same catalytic efficiency that the native enzyme has with NADH. This allowed them to couple the amino acid dehydrogenase to an alcohol dehydrogenase and an aminotransferase to efficiently produce dicyclic diamines from the corresponding dicyclic dialcohols. In order to obtain this variant, homology models of both the
                      - emphasis [ref=e1634]: Bacillus subtilis
                      - text: enzyme and alanine dehydrogenase of
                      - emphasis [ref=e1635]: Shewanella
                      - text: ", which was known to be able to use both NADH and NADPH as cofactors, were generated based on the crystal structure of"
                      - emphasis [ref=e1636]: Mycobacterium tuberculosis
                      - text: alanine dehydrogenase
                      - link "[178]" [ref=e1637] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0890"
                        - generic [ref=e1638]: "[178]"
                      - text: . Then, mutations were introduced in the
                      - emphasis [ref=e1639]: Bacillus subtilis
                      - text: enzyme to mimic the cofactor binding pocket of the
                      - emphasis [ref=e1640]: Shewanella
                      - text: enzyme
                      - link "[179]" [ref=e1641] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0895"
                        - generic [ref=e1642]: "[179]"
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1643]:
                      - text: Another frequent goal when engineering oxidoreductases is the modification of substrate specificity. There are several examples, often concerning alcohol dehydrogenases, where variants with activity towards a new substrate or a different isomer of one of the natural substrates were generated. For example, Laadan et al. identified a mutant of alcohol dehydrogenase 1 (ADH1) of
                      - emphasis [ref=e1644]: Saccharomyces cerevisiae
                      - text: able to catalyze the NADH-dependent reduction of 5-hydroxymethylfurfural to 2,5-bis-hydroxymethylfuran, which could enhance the production of bioethanol through fermentation of lignocellulosic substrates by
                      - emphasis [ref=e1645]: Saccharomyces cerevisiae
                      - text: . In order to do so,
                      - emphasis [ref=e1646]: Saccharomyces cerevisiae
                      - text: cells where the ADH1 gene had been deleted were transformed with the library of variants and selected by their ability to grow anaerobically, and then activity assays for the reduction of 5-HMF were performed
                      - link "[180]" [ref=e1647] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0900"
                        - generic [ref=e1648]: "[180]"
                      - text: . In another study, Rellos et al. generated a library of variants of
                      - emphasis [ref=e1649]: Zymomonas mobilis
                      - text: alcohol dehydrogenase 2, an NADH-dependent, iron-activated alcohol dehydrogenase able to oxidize only ethanol, 1-propanol and allyl alcohol. The library of variants was generated by a combination of error-prone PCR and site-directed mutagenesis, and transformants were screened by a colorimetric assay with butanol. Variants with higher activity towards butanol than to ethanol, as well as variants able to use NADPH/NADP
                      - superscript [ref=e1650]: +
                      - text: while still retaining the ability to use NADH/NAD
                      - superscript [ref=e1651]: +
                      - text: ", were identified. Additionally, another variant was able to use Zn instead of Fe as the metallic cofactor"
                      - link "[181]" [ref=e1652] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0905"
                        - generic [ref=e1653]: "[181]"
                      - text: . There have also been several cases where the enantiospecificity of an alcohol dehydrogenase has been altered. However, most frequently the reversal of enantiospecificity is not total, comes at the cost of decreased catalytic efficiency or produces unexpected side-effects. For example, Akita et al. generated a thermostable NADPH-dependent d-amino acid dehydrogenase (DAADH) from
                      - emphasis [ref=e1654]: Ureibacillus thermosphaericus
                      - text: meso-diaminopimelate dehydrogenase (DAPDH) by introducing five point mutations in the active site that had been previously described to be responsible for converting another DAPDH from a mesophilic organism (
                      - emphasis [ref=e1655]: Corynebacterium glutamicum
                      - text: ) into a DAADH
                      - link "[182]" [ref=e1656] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0910"
                        - generic [ref=e1657]: "[182]"
                      - text: . Interestingly, while the resulting variant was able to oxidize several d-amino acids, it was no longer able to oxidize meso-diaminopimelate
                      - link "[183]" [ref=e1658] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0915"
                        - generic [ref=e1659]: "[183]"
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1660]:
                      - text: Finally, another quality usually sought during the screening of variant libraries is increased thermal stability. Increasing thermal stability is not only desirable for achieving a reduced loss of catalyst during enzyme-catalyzed reactions, but also because the catalyzed reaction takes place at a higher rate when temperature is increased if the enzyme can withstand the higher temperature. For example, a dramatic increase in the thermal stability of phosphite dehydrogenase was achieved by Johannes et al., who identified a mutant with a T50 (required temperature to reduce the initial enzyme activity by 50%) higher than the wild type by 20 °C and a 7000-fold greater half-life at 45 °C, which could be used as an efficient way to regenerate reduced NAD(P)H in industrial biocatalysis applications. In this case, the approach involved a combination of error-prone PCR and site-directed mutagenesis. Three rounds of error-prone PCR were carried out, starting with the wild type gene. After each round of error-prone PCR, variants with improved thermal stability were identified. The best variants were sequenced, and the mutations found in all variants were incorporated into the template. The new template incorporating all the mutations served as the template for the next round of error-prone PCR
                      - link "[184]" [ref=e1661] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0920"
                        - generic [ref=e1662]: "[184]"
                      - text: .
                    - generic [ref=e1663]:
                      - text: Many attempts at modifying the substrate and cofactor specificity of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases have been carried out and these attempts have been successful to a certain extent. However, in many cases the mutations do not achieve the desired effect, cause the loss of catalytic activity or lead to unexpected results
                      - link "[185]" [ref=e1664] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0925"
                        - generic [ref=e1665]: "[185]"
                      - text: ","
                      - link "[186]" [ref=e1666] [cursor=pointer]:
                        - /url: "#bb0930"
                        - generic [ref=e1667]: "[186]"
                      - text: . This is partially due to the lack of understanding of the relationship between the amino acid sequence of a protein, its structure and its function, which still prevents us from developing widely applicable methodologies to alter the substrate or cofactor specificity of oxidoreductases.
                - generic [ref=e1668]:
                  - heading "6. Conclusion and future perspective" [level=2] [ref=e1669]
                  - generic [ref=e1670]: NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases constitute an extremely diverse group of enzymes able to act on a similarly vast range of substrates to catalyze electron transfer reactions. In recent years there have been a growing number of studies where novel applications based on these enzymes are proposed, or new methods proposed where these enzymes or organisms containing them can replace other less advantageous methods for catalysis.
                  - generic [ref=e1671]:
                    - text: Various factors underpin ongoing progress in research towards applications based on NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases. The explosive growth in available genomic information about all types of organisms has proven to be very useful for the identification of NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductases, increasing the number of members of known types and thereby improving understanding of conserved features, but also leading to identification of new types such as the ATP and NADPH-dependent CAR activity described in
                    - link "section 3" [ref=e1672] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#s0015"
                      - generic [ref=e1673]: section 3
                    - text: ", EC 1.2.1. In parallel, the acceleration in the rate at which protein structures are determined and made public is providing more insight into the structural basis of aspects such as cofactor and substrate specificity, information which is important to guide protein engineering. Furthermore, the ability to exploit this increasing knowledge base has been facilitated by the development of protein engineering and directed evolution approaches which allow the properties of enzymes to be tailored in rational and semi-rational ways. There has been practical progress in the prediction of enzyme function from structure: The new CSR-SALAD computational tool uses recently-established rules to interpret structural and sequence information in order to predict specific mutations which might allow reversal of the cofactor specificity of a given NAD(P)H-dependent oxidoreductase"
                    - link "[66]" [ref=e1674] [cursor=pointer]:
                      - /url: "#bb0330"
                      - generic [ref=e1675]: "[66]"
                    - text: . The rapid growth in sequence and structural information together with technological developments will undoubtedly expand still further the potential and usefulness of an already naturally versatile group of enzymes.
                - generic [ref=e1676]:
